#166. Is a water leak detector worth it? Here’s what home experts say

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NBC Selected independently determines what we cover and recommend. When you click on or buy through our links, we earn a commission. Learn more.

With all the content available online these days, it can be hard to figure out what’s real and what’s hype. While we don’t always have the answers, we have a direct line to people who do — which is why we present to you our series The Internet Made Me Ask, where we have experts break down the truth about some of the buzziest trends (and questions) on our timelines, like whether bath sheets are actually different from towels.

It’s expected that homeowners have things like smoke and carbon monoxide detectors in their homes to keep them and their families safe. But another type of home-related detector that’s also worth considering is a water leak detector. Depending on where you live and what type of home you have, a water leak detector can be a very beneficial tool, since it can help track water pressure and temperature, and help you get ahead of frozen pipes in the winter, which can be a major inconvenience — and a costly one if the pipes burst.

To learn more about how water leak detectors actually work and how to shop for one, I spoke to Lou Manfredini, a seasoned home expert at Ace Hardware*, who says that there are several different types of detectors to consider.

*Any inclusion of products sold at Ace Hardware in this story was made independently of Manfredini, who we only consulted for guidance. We did not ask Manfredini for product recommendations.

Want more from NBC Selected? Sign up for our newsletter, The Selection, and shop smarter.

All of the products below have at least a four-star rating from 500 or more shoppers on Amazon (or 100 or more at another retailer if it’s unavailable at Amazon).

This water leak detector from GoveeLife includes a set of three water leak sensors that can detect when water starts pooling around it, such as under the pipe of a sink, for example. It also includes a WiFi adapter, since it’s a smart device that connects to the brand’s app on your phone, where it can alert you of a leak — it will also send you an email notification and/or a text message. You’ll also get a set of six AAA batteries. The WiFi hub and the water leak sensors also operate within 1,800 feet of each other, so you can use them in very large homes or buildings.

This water leak detector works with Amazon Alexa to send you notifications if it senses water or flooding on the ground, so if you’re away during a storm, for example, it can let you know water has entered your basement, bathroom or living room. It comes with an adapter and a power cable with a sensor connected to it. It detects water drops as well as if it’s been submerged in water, according to the brand. It’s one of the most affordable options on this list and a great one to try if you’re new water leak detectors and have a small budget.

This water leak detector connects directly to the pipes of your home’s water supply. It fits pipes of different diameters and materials, including brass, copper and PVC. Additionally, this water leak detector doesn’t require installation by a professional and only takes about five minutes to connect, according to the brand. The device can also alert you when there’s a change in water flow inside the pipes, not just a leak going outward.

This water leak detector from Moen monitors both water floor and temperature in the water going through the pipes in your home’s water supply. Similar to the Hydrific detector, it attaches to the pipe and can send an alert straight to your phone via Moen’s Smart Water Network app. It also detects temperature changes in water (not just flow), so it can help prevent your pipes from freezing by turning off the water if the temperature drops below a certain degree. While you can technically install it yourself, the brand recommends having a professional do it.

Water leak detectors, also sometimes called water leak sensors (which are technically a different type of detector), are systems that monitor water activity in your home. “There are several types, but basically they sense when water is present and will sound an alarm or send you a notification to your phone or computer to alert you of a leak,” says Manfredini. “The basic leak detectors are placed in areas where there may be a concern for water leaking.”

This includes areas like under a sink, under a water heater or in a basement, according to Manfredini. He further explains that they essentially watch for what seems like an excessive use of water, which can sometimes be caused by a burst pipe or a running toilet.

If it’s a point-of-use detector, it can literally sense when water is dripping on it or if it’s pooling around the device on the floor or the surface of a cabinet under a faucet for example. An in-line water detector connects to your pipes to determine changes in flow and temperature (more on this below).

There are a few different types of water leak detectors, but in general, you’ll find that many come in one of two forms, according to Chris Petri, owner of Petri Plumbing, Heating, Cooling & Drain Cleaning. These are the types you typically see:

“Every home benefits from proactive leak detection,” says Petri. “Waiting until a leak is visually noticeable often means damage has already occurred, especially since leaks frequently happen in hidden areas or while you are away.”

Getting a water leak detector that works for your type of space is important in determining if it’s a worthy investment, so it needs to have certain features. “Key features to consider include automatic water shutoff capabilities, smart notifications (such as app alerts), placement requirements, and whether you need whole-home coverage (in-line) or targeted area protection (point-of-use).”

At NBC Selected, we work with experts who have specialized knowledge and authority based on relevant training and/or experience. We also take steps to ensure all expert advice and recommendations are made independently and without undisclosed financial conflicts of interest.

*Any inclusion of products sold at Ace Hardware in this story was made independently of Manfredini, who we only consulted for guidance. We did not ask Manfredini for product recommendations.

#232. Israel's election deadlock is a sign of the country's deep divisions

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"Democracy is the worst form of government except for all those other forms that have been tried." So said Winston Churchill in the House of Commons in 1947.

His aphorism came to mind as Israel's election results filtered through on Tuesday night - exit polls blending with ballots that were cast to dash or fulfil people's hopes and fears.

This election has, in some ways, been a celebration of democracy. Voters turning out in record numbers, Jews and Arabs alike. Many spent thousands to fly in from abroad to cast their ballot.

At polling stations across the country, we saw the passion of campaigners, as they used every minute to try to change that last mind and win that last vote.

President Isaac Herzog said the election was a "badge of honour" for Israeli society. "It is remarkable to see how deep the desire for democracy is in Israel," he exclaimed.

Yet this election left the most established democracy in the Middle East without a clear path to a new government. It produced an inconclusive result where neither opposing bloc will have a majority in parliament.

Some might say this was business as usual.

Israel has never had a one-party government. Its fragmented, proportional representation system is almost designed to govern by coalition.

Yet many commentators warned this election had revealed the fundamental and perhaps growing divisions that scar this nation. This was not a plebiscite in which an electorate united around a single cause.

Israel's election result is messy - here's why

Some of these fissures are not new. There were divisions over judicial reforms that led to mass demonstrations in the early years of this government.

There were also divisions over how to respond to the deadly 7 October 2023 attacks - whether to save hostages or destroy Hamas. The casualties suffered by Israeli reservists in the fighting in Gaza that followed deepened tensions over the exemption of ultra-Orthodox young men from military service.

But has this election put in even sharper contrast the deep fault-lines that lie within Israeli society?

There are the tensions between the religious and the secular, divides not just of faith but of society.

Should Israel's government subsidise religious study? Should some parents sacrifice their sons and daughters to defend Israel when others do not? The ultra-Orthodox now make up almost 15% of the population. Their world-view is poles apart from that of denizens of high-tech, modern neighbourhoods of Tel Aviv.

There are divisions over fundamental politics.

Benjamin Netanyahu has called for "a strong right-wing national government"

Benjamin Netanyahu's Likud party suffered significant losses, but is still on track to be the largest in the Knesset. All this for a prime minister accused of corruption, which he denies; a leader also accused of failing to protect Israel before 7 October, something he will not allow to be investigated.

Some 10% of voters backed the Jewish supremacist far-right: politicians like Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich, who even as results were being announced was promising to establish Jewish settlements in Gaza.

The Arab parties, which represent about a fifth of Israeli citizens, made huge gains, winning some 15% of the vote, potentially giving them seats numbering in the high teens.

And then there was the opposition bloc, a mix of centre, left and right led by Gadi Eisenkot who fell short of winning the change they wanted - change that many believed was existential for the future of this country.

Gadi Eisenkot declared that the public had "spoken in favour of change"

How these vastly diverging views coalesce into some form of government is now the unenviable task facing Israel's body politic - the choices and compromises they may have to make could involve fundamental decisions about the country's future.

Would Netanyahu be ready to abandon his religious allies and form a coalition with right-wingers from the opposition bloc? Would they be ready to swallow their hostility towards the prime minister and sit in his government?

Is the opposition bloc ready to abandon much of its campaign promises and co-operate with Arab parties to force Netanyahu out of office? Are the Arab parties ready to risk joining a government that could fall within months?

Yair Golan, the left-wing Democrat leader, urged the opposition bloc to form a partnership with Arab parties. "We must no longer accept Netanyahu's racist division between Jewish votes and Arab votes," he said.

But are the people of Israel ready for such a reality?

Amit Segal, an Israeli commentator sympathetic towards Netanyahu, said the high turnout among Arab Israeli voters "reminded Jews on both sides that, after years of division, they may have to learn to sit together after all".

The risk is that this election, instead of putting Israel on a new path, has instead raised further doubts about what it is that unites its citizens. What are the common factors that bind this complex, varied collection of peoples into one polity?

Autocratic governments, which are growing in number, have long criticised democracies for producing weak and temporary governments, often fragile coalitions that struggle to hold together, let alone deliver what the people wanted.

Israel may now struggle for months to form a government. It might even fail and have to hold another election.

President Herzog acknowledged this risk head on as he prepared to open coalition negotiations: "We must do everything we can, certainly after yesterday, to avoid another round of elections."

For years, Israel has focused on defending itself against its external enemies. But must it also now be ready to defend itself from perils within, if the divisions writ large by this election become unmanageable?

In another speech, Churchill once said democracy was not just "insurance against dangers from abroad". It was also, he added, "insurance against dangers scarcely less grave and much more near and constant which threaten us here at home".

Can Israel's democracy live up to this challenge?

The bitter row over military service that could shape Israel's future

How could Israel break election deadlock? Here are four scenarios

#189. Google is aware its AI sometimes won't answer who won the 2020 election: 'We're fixing the issue'

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google's AI Mode was supposed to reimagine search. Users are noticing that it's struggling with a basic question: "Who won the 2020 election?"

On Wednesday, users on BlueSky and other social media platforms took notice that Google's AI Mode wouldn't give a straight answer about who won the White House in 2020.

"To verify the winner, certified outcomes, electoral votes, and popular vote totals for the 2020 United States presidential election, you can review the official reports published by the Federal Election Commission (FEC)," the AI Mode replied to a Business Insider reporter who asked, "Who won the 2020 presidential election in the United States?"

When other Business Insider reporters asked similar questions, the AI Mode sometimes responded that President Joe Biden had won. Google Gemini, the search giant's AI system, which is accessed via a different site than AI mode, responded to a similar question that Biden was the winner.

It's not entirely clear why Google tells users to review the results published by the Federal Election Commission. While the FEC does publish the results of presidential elections, the commission itself has no role in the certification of state election results or the formal counting of Electoral College votes.

On Wednesday afternoon, Google said it was working to fix the issue, which it attributed to a "technical gap."

"Our systems verify election responses using news articles or AP data for recent elections, and historic records for older ones," Google wrote on X. "There was a technical gap with 2020 - same is true for 2022 Senate races, which we're also working to fix."

For years, President Donald Trump has refused to concede that he lost to Biden. After his campaign lost legal challenges, Trump and his allies suggested that lawmakers themselves could change the outcome, fueling the January 6, 2021, Capitol riot. The riot briefly delayed the congressional verification of Biden's victory.

The user posted screenshots that show AI mode responding to questions about who won other presidential elections. When a Business Insider reporter ran similar queries, AI mode sometimes said who had won previous presidential elections, and other times it did not.

Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.

The 2026 midterms are likely to test tech and AI companies, many of which have announced policies to limit users' abuse of their tools.

OpenAI recently announced that its model would produce "stylized — also known as cartoonified — illustrations, rather than photorealistic images" if asked to produce images "depicting election interference or that may discourage voting." In June, Meta announced that it would add "more information to ads we detect have been created with third-party AI tools." And Google previously said it was "rolling out additional protections to limit the creation of misleading election-related images."

When asked why it could not say who won the 2020 election, the AI Mode responded, "Because recent election results can be a sensitive civic topic, guiding you directly to primary historical databases prevents any perception of bias or mischaracterization of the official public record."

#287. Apple's PROLED XDR Display has a cringy name but incredible specs

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The displays offer high brightness, contrast and color accuracy and even adapt to pixel usage.

With all the fuss about Apple finally releasing a touchscreen MacBook Pro, you may have forgotten about the new model's other key display technology: OLED, or what Apple calls PROLED XDR. It's typical of Apple to change a standard tech term to a cringy marketing-focused name, but in this case, the "PRO" designation appears to be justified.

The new MacBook Pros are squarely aimed at photographers, artists and video editors who can justify the high price for a high-quality touch display. To appeal to those folks, Apple didn't just integrate a touch feature and OLED display and call it a day. Rather, it built what looks like one of the best pro laptop displays ever, with high brightness and contrast, excellent color accuracy and even a display engine that adapts to a pixel's age.

For the new models, Apple used tandem OLED technology (first employed on the M4 iPad Pro) that stacks multiple organic layers into a single display panel. Judging by an Apple graphic in its press release, it appears to use a triangular pixel arrangement and not a striped RGB OLED system that can offer sharper text (we've reached out to the company for clarification on that).

There are several key benefits to tandem OLED tech, namely high peak HDR brightness, a lower burn-in risk and a longer panel lifespan. It's also brighter and more color-accurate when viewed from an angle. At the same time, it retains and even improves the primary benefit of OLED displays: extremely high contrast.

Apple says that the new MacBook Pros with PROLED XDR deliver up to 2,000 nits peak HDR, compared to 1,600 nits peak HDR for current models with Mini LED tech. That's very high for an OLED display and at least as bright or brighter than any other OLED laptop on the market (if accurate). Apple also touts an "industry-leading" 10,000,000:1 contrast ratio, much higher than current MacBook Pro models.

The PROLED XDR screens also offer high color accuracy. Apple says the new models now cover both the P3 (HDR) and Adobe RGB color gamuts "with notably wider coverage in green and cyan ranges." They cover 80 percent of the Rec.2020 gamut for HDR video editing and color grading, an impressive figure for a laptop display.

The company also touts "cutting-edge factory calibration" to ensure luminance, white point and color gamut align with standard targets, making it useful for "filmmaking, design and even medical imaging," Apple claims. Its advanced display engine also controls each pixel's color and luminance "at the sub-millisecond level" to compensate for content, temperature and environment. That tech also addresses a key problem with OLED (organic pixel degradation over time), by adapting each pixel's age and usage.

The new laptops use Apple's 120Hz ProMotion display for reasonably high refresh rates when gaming or creating content. And if you work outside at times or in a bright environment, you can get the new models with Apple's nano-texture option that's "now precisely etched into the cover glass," Apple says, to further reduce glare.

The new MacBook Pros with PROLED XDR touch displays probably aren't worth the higher $400 base price for most people. With all the tech it's crammed into the displays, though, the models will be awfully tempting for users who make their money by creating content.

This week's buyout is getting a lot less uptake, according to the company.

#386. Most Servers on the Planet Are Vulnerable to This Firmware Code-Execution Flaw

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Defenders across the world face the burden of responding to yet another botched code-execution vulnerability, this time in boot-time firmware used by most of the world’s server vendors and data centers.

The vulnerability, with a severity rating of 9.5 out of 10, resides in the TianoCore EDK II, an open source codebase that hardware makers and cloud providers use to create the UEFI firmware that runs during the boot-up sequence. Last year, a representative of the UEFI Forum called EDK2 the “preeminent solution / implementation of the UEFI specifications.” Virtually all UEFI suppliers (sometimes still called IBVs or independent BIOS vendors), including AMI, Insyde, and Phoenix, use the reference code extensively. Project Mu, Microsoft’s hardened EDK2 fork, used the code containing the vulnerable component as well.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-97631, is a memory-corruption flaw in an ICMP component that attackers can exploit to execute malicious code that runs before the operating system and firmware can implement defenses like Secure Boot, which establishes a chain of trust for all code, including the Trusted Platform Module (TPM), loaded during boot. While final payloads delivered by CVE-2026-97631 have no persistence—meaning they run only once—they can give attackers a wide opening to install other payloads that also run each time early in the boot process.

The parties that discovered CVE-2026-97631—Alex Matrosov of Anthropic and Dan Holmes of Google—had arranged for it to stay under wraps until a coordinated disclosure that was scheduled for January. AMI, a maker of motherboards and other hardware, published this pull request last month that detailed the vulnerability and included a code fix. Once the post came to the attention of the discovering parties, TianoCore Project members published a public advisory last week.

With publicly available patch code, it will be trivial for advanced hackers to learn how the vulnerability is triggered and to build working exploits. Further compounding the urgency, patching UEFI code vulnerabilities in millions of end devices sold and used by thousands of hardware makers and cloud providers is cumbersome and time-consuming. Word of the vulnerability comes six days after another botched disclosure, this one involving a critical code-execution vulnerability in the PHP programming language.

“This vulnerability in the ICMP parser of the EDK2 network stack is interesting in terms of how it affects the chain of trust and the broader ecosystem,” said HD Moore, creator of Metasploit and chief research officer at security firm Dragos. “An attacker intent on compromising a nearby endpoint or server could use this bug to hijack program flow at a point where the boot process still has access to sensitive components, like the endpoint’s TPM. This could be used to break disk encryption for cases where an attacker has short-term network access (and it would be even easier in a theft scenario).”

CVE-2026-97631 is a 16-year-old buffer overflow in the PXE-Base Code driver (NetworkPkg/UefiPxeBcDxe), which is responsible for allowing servers, storage equipment, and other devices to boot using code downloaded from a local network. During this process—known as the pre-execution environment boot—the driver uses a network listener to monitor for ICMP errors. Because the driver doesn’t enforce a memory copy limit of 504 bytes, overwriting adjacent data, a malicious insider or a hacker who has gained low-level access to a local network can send an error message that appends code past this limit. The IUEFI module running on the CPU then becomes corrupted.

During network boot, the PXE Base Code driver (NetworkPkg/UefiPxeBcDxe) keeps a receive request open for ICMP error messages and copies each received message into the fixed 504-byte field Mode.IcmpError of its private context structure. The copy loop’s bounds handling is incorrect in both the IPv4 and IPv6 handlers (PxeBcIcmpErrorDpcHandle and PxeBcIcmp6ErrorDpcHandle): for a message longer than 504 bytes it still copies bytes into the field, and it advances the destination pointer by the cumulative rather than the per-fragment length, so an oversized message writes attacker-controlled bytes past the field. Nothing earlier on the receive path restricts the message length.

The malicious error can be delivered through a simple unicast sent from any network distance to a target device’s IP address, as long as it’s sent during the PXE boot sequence. An attacker can deliver a denial-of-service by causing the boot sequence to hang. When the attacker knows what the target firmware build is, it can plant a “counterfeit event” that executes the malicious code on the CPU in its UEFI firmware environment. The code runs in ring 0, a CPU’s most privileged execution level. Because exploits bypass attestation processes designed to cryptographically prove to remote servers that the hardware is secure, they present a key opportunity for elevating limited privileges.

CVE-2026-97631 joins a long list of previously discovered vulnerabilities in UEFI or code that works closely with it. The most severe of them, LogoFail, stemmed from logo images included in the UEFI of just about every hardware maker. Rendering bugs allowed attackers to subvert the chain of trust UEFI enforces and go on to install malicious components before the OS loaded or Secure Boot ran. PixieFail was a vulnerability similar to CVE-2026-97631. It, too, was the result of faulty PXE boot flaws in EDK2. Following discovery of the vulnerability, researchers embarked on multiple fuzzing projects designed to surface other critical flaws in the open source code library. It appears those efforts failed to find this latest flaw.

Other recently discovered UEFI flaws include:

Like CVE-2026-97631, exploitation or use of these payloads is appealing because it allows attackers to infect machines early in the boot process, before TPM and other defenses load, so they can inflict maximum damage. Given the ubiquity of CVE-2026-97631, Moore said it’s likely a “forever” bug that will live on in end-of-support systems.

There are important caveats and limitations to exploiting this latest vulnerability. As noted, some level of control over a local network is a prerequisite. That likely limits exploitation to insiders or attackers, such as nation-states or ransomware operators, who manage to gain low-level access inside a network and are looking for a way to elevate system rights. There’s no evidence yet that CVE-2026-97631 provides an initial entry point into a network. There are other hurdles to clear.

“For exploiting the vulnerability itself, an attacker needs to guess specific addresses within the target system, so it’s not something that you can throw a single exploit at any target and get RCE across a diverse range of devices, but it needs to be tuned for each target model/version/configuration since the addresses will change based on those,” Jesse Michael, a principal researcher at firmware security firm Eclypsium, told Ars.

“Unlike some of the previous, high-profile vulnerabilities like BootHole, Black Lotus, and Baton Drop, this vulnerability can be fixed with a basic code fix, which will require deploying a BIOS/UEFI update, but they don’t need to deal with revocations, pk/kek/db/dbx key management updates, or making sure to update OS and provisioning media like you do with Secure Boot bypasses in the bootloader itself.”

CVE-2026-97631 doesn’t affect home and small office users, or any other users who boot their devices solely from disk drives. Instead, it applies only to organizations with machines that boot from local networks, or conceivably from Internet-connected resources. Even then, the targeted firmware must rely on EDK2, as opposed to a different reference implementation, and use the PXE functionality (which is turned on by default).

In an email, Intel said that none of its “commercial products currently under security support” are known to be affected. “Intel does not include the affected EDK2 functionality in its reference code, though it may be integrated by an OEM board vendor or system manufacturer,” the company added. It advised customers to check with their system manufacturer for specific guidance. Publicly available Intel reference code, however, makes reference to EDK2. Further, TianoCore has said that each Intel-supplied UEFI development kit “is a fully validated implementation … for a specific revision of the EDK II source.” Many other Intel documents reference EDK2 as well.

Arm, meanwhile, responded to a list of questions with: “CVE-2026-97631 is not directly applicable to Arm’s product portfolio.” Like Intel, firmware the company makes available also borrows heavily from EDK2. TianoCore has also documented Arm’s reliance on EDK2.

While it’s possible some platforms are free of EDK2 code, it’s prudent for users of all servers to investigate if CVE-2026-97631 affects them.

Questions sent to AMD, Phoenix Technologies, AMI, Microsoft, and Amazon—likely some of the other biggest organizations affected—went unanswered when this post went live. Without concrete answers to questions about the use of EDK2 by organizations like these, it’s impossible to say what or how devices are affected and what actions, if any, these organizations, or their users, should take. Representatives from the TianoCore Project also didn’t respond.

Some readers are likely to be unhappy at the lack of detail. Sometimes—particularly in cases involving botched disclosures that break embargoes months before they’re scheduled to lift—there’s no escaping the lack of actionable information in initial reporting.

Those affected should check with the maker of the firmware being used to boot their systems and ask for guidance. Beyond that, they should consider turning off network boot capabilities at least temporarily if possible. Firmware configurations should always enable security features such as TPM and Secure Boot whenever possible, although those defenses don’t protect against this particular threat. It’s also a good idea to monitor logs for signs of exploitation attempts.

#30. Netanyahu's bloc leading Israel election but short of majority, partial results show

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Israel's election result is messy - here's why

Partial results from Israel's general election show veteran Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu is leading his rival, former military chief Gadi Eisenkot, but is still short of a majority in parliament.

With 85% of ballots counted, Netanyahu's Likud party and its far-right and ultra-Orthodox Jewish allies were on track to win 53 seats in the 120-seat Knesset. Eisenkot's Yashar party and three other Jewish opposition parties in his bloc were set for 48.

Both leaders have said they will try to form a governing coalition.

Arab-led parties, who are on course to win an unprecedented 19 seats, could emerge as kingmakers, but all but one of the Jewish parties have pledged not to work with them.

With between 430,000 and 480,000 absentee ballots from groups including soldiers, diplomats and hospital patients yet to be counted, it could still take a day or two for the final election results to emerge.

Once they are confirmed, President Isaac Herzog will hold consultations with all the political factions and invite the leader whom he believes has the best chance of success to form a government - a process that could take weeks.

If they fail, then ultimately there would have to be another election.

How could Israel break election deadlock? Here are four scenarios

Netanyahu and Eisenkot face difficult paths to power as neither set to win majority

During a meeting at his residence in Jerusalem, Herzog praised the 77.7% turnout in Tuesday's election, which was the country's highest in more than two decades.

"It is a badge of honour for Israeli society, and it was remarkable to see how deep the desire for democracy is in Israel," he said.

He added that he expected to receive the official results next week, after which he would begin discussions with every faction.

"We must do everything we can, certainly after yesterday, to avoid another round of elections. So, I expect everyone who comes here to come with an open mind."

Both the prime minister and his main rival have insisted that they should be tasked with forming a governing coalition.

Benjamin Netanyahu called for "a strong right-wing national government"

Eisenkot declared that the public had "spoken in favour of change", after initial exit polls published by three Israeli broadcasters projected that his "Change Bloc" would emerge with between 52 and 53 seats in the Knesset, compared to 50 seats for Netanyahu's bloc.

"The coalition of failure has lost the people's trust," he told supporters at Yashar's election headquarters in Tel Aviv. "I will work with my partners to ensure that the people's decision is translated into a Zionist government."

Later, the broadcasters updated their exit polls, with two projecting that the rival blocs would be tied on 51 seats and one giving Netanyahu's bloc a narrow three-seat lead.

Netanyahu then appeared at Likud's headquarters in Jerusalem and declared that his right-wing bloc had won a "great majority".

"We must put in place a strong right-wing national government," he said. "It is forbidden, simply forbidden, to endanger Israel by forming a government that relies on anti-Zionist Arab parties."

As the votes were counted on Wednesday, it emerged that his bloc had done better than the exit polls predicted and was on course to win 53 seats. However, that figure is 15 seats fewer than it had once controlled in the last Knesset.

Tuesday's election was the first since the deadly Hamas-led attacks on Israel on 7 October 2023 and the wars that followed in Gaza, Lebanon and Iran.

It was also seen by Israelis as one of the most consequential in the country's history, because it could decide not just their leadership but whether the country continues on its current hard-line, religious-nationalist trajectory.

Key campaign issues included accountability for the security failures that led to the 7 October attacks, the conduct of regional wars, conscripting ultra-Orthodox Jewish men into the military, judicial reforms and Israel's international standing.

Avigail Levine from Kfar Saba, who voted for former prime minister Naftali Bennett's opposition Beyachad party, told the BBC that she was disappointed with the election results.

"I was hoping for new leadership. I feel we need it as a nation," she said. "The people in power on 7 October should not be in power now."

She said people were frustrated that Netanyahu had not shouldered responsibility for what happened three years ago, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage. Her husband's grandmother was among those who died in Kibbutz Be'eri.

Gal from Ramat Gan said he voted for Netanyahu because Israelis "cannot relax and live in peace with neighbours when all they want is to erase us from the map".

He also said he was "afraid of the strengthening of the Arab political parties".

Likud complained on Wednesday that they had "stolen" the election. It alleged that they had resorted to "bribery, threats, violence, fraud and the use of prohibited software to encourage voters", without providing any evidence. The Central Elections Committee said it would examine the claims as part of a post-election review.

Gadi Eisenkot said the Israeli public had "spoken in favour of change"

The two Arab parties - the United Arab List and the Joint List - are set to almost double their representation because of a surge in turnout among Arab Israelis, who account for about a fifth of the population and often describe themselves as Palestinian citizens of Israel.

Mahdi Kabaha who lives in a village near Haifa and campaigned to get out the Arab vote, described the result as "historic".

"Arab citizens came out and said clearly: we want a real say in this country," he said.

The head of the Joint List, Yousef Jabareen, told the BBC that Arab voters had been motivated in part by the opportunity to remove far-right National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir from office, whom he accused of racism and "provocation against our community".

Ben-Gvir, the leader of the Jewish Power party, has called for the annexation of the occupied West Bank, Jewish settlement of Gaza, and the "voluntary emigration" of Palestinians. Several Western countries have imposed sanctions on him for incitement against Palestinians.

United Arab List leader Mansour Abbas, who served in a short-lived governing coalition formed after the 2021 elections, said on Tuesday night that he hoped "the numbers will work out and we, the Change Bloc and the UAL, can ensure that there are 61 Knesset seats".

The leader of the left-wing Democrats party, Yair Golan, told his partners in Eisenkot's bloc that there was an "opportunity to form a change government in partnership" with the Arab parties and replace Netanyahu's coalition.

"We have no future here if we don't stop being afraid. And if we don't put an end to racism," he wrote on X.

Eisenkot has previously raised objections or attached conditions to that kind of alliance, including recognising Israel as Jewish and democratic, and Hamas as a terrorist organisation that must be destroyed. The right-wing parties in his bloc, meanwhile, have flatly rejected it.

"The opposition parties cannot dictate any conditions," Jabareen said. "If the opposition in Israel are willing to afford real and substantive change, and an alternative for the dangerous, extreme-right-wing government... they will have to negotiate with us."

#209. A Ukrainian war robot used AI to complete a 12-mile supply run almost entirely on its own. The unit says it's a combat first.

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Ukrainian military unit said on Wednesday that it successfully sent a ground robot on a 12-mile supply run, using AI to navigate for nearly the entirety of the mission.

The Lava Unmanned Systems Regiment of the 2nd Corps, or Khartiia Corps, of Ukraine's National Guard announced the completion of the autonomous logistics mission in a statement shared with Business Insider, calling it the "first of its kind under combat conditions."

The unit said that the robot completed 93% of its 20-kilometer trip on its own, delivering cargo and returning to its starting point autonomously, with an operator intervening for only 1.4 kilometers of the journey.

Before departure, a separate ground robot equipped with light detection and ranging technology, or LiDAR, mapped the route and terrain in 3D. Cameras on the supply bot detected obstacles while AI-powered navigation guided the robot.

"This mission has shown that autonomous ground systems are already capable of operating in real combat conditions," said Andrii Kopach, who serves as commander of the Lava Regiment's Unmanned Ground Systems Battalion.

"The biggest change is that the operator is no longer a driver but a dispatcher," he said. "Instead of controlling the vehicle every second, they monitor its movement and step in only when needed."

Kopach said that the unit's next ambitions are to enable a single operator to oversee multiple robots at once and expand autonomous missions to casualty evacuations. He added that more AI-powered ground robots would reduce dependence on "a constant control link," such as Starlink, a satellite internet service which recently saw major speed disruptions in Ukraine, negatively affecting front-line operations.

Ground robots have become an important tool for Ukraine, carrying out resupply, casualty evacuation, and assault missions. The Ukrainian military, and Western military leaders studying lessons from the war, hope that increased reliance on ground robots and aerial drones, as well as the increased integration of AI capabilities into these systems, can help reduce the risks to the troops who would otherwise perform those missions on increasingly dangerous battlefields.

#305. Microsoft reveals strong cloud growth amid financial reporting overhaul

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Windows and Xbox revenues have declined during Microsoft’s first quarter of the 2027 financial year.

Windows and Xbox revenues have declined during Microsoft’s first quarter of the 2027 financial year.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft just posted the first quarter of its 2027 fiscal financial results. The software maker made $92.3 billion in revenue and a net income of $33.6 billion during Q1. Revenue is up 19 percent, and net income has increased by 32 percent.

It’s a new financial year for Microsoft, so the company is changing up how it reports its finances by splitting its businesses into two segments for the first time. Gone are the previous segments: Productivity and Business Processes, Intelligent Cloud, and More Personal Computing. It’s now just Agents and Infra, and Devices and Consumer.

Agents and Infra, which includes businesses like Azure, Microsoft 365 commercial and consumer, and server products, makes up 83 percent ($76.8 billion) of the overall $92.3 billion of Microsoft’s revenue and has increased 25 percent year-over-year. The big changes to Microsoft’s financial reporting also mean we will now see the company disclose Azure revenue on a quarterly basis. Azure contributed $33.17 billion in revenue this quarter, up 48 percent year-over-year.

Devices and Consumer, which includes Windows, Xbox, Surface, and Microsoft’s search and advertising businesses, makes up the remaining 17 percent ($15.5 billion) of Microsoft’s $92.3 billion in revenues this quarter — a decrease of three percent.

Windows OEM and devices revenue is down 22 percent year-over-year, amid a RAMageddon crisis that has seen the overall PC market dropped by 20 percent in the recent quarter. Microsoft blames the revenue drop on the benefit of Windows 10 end of support during the prior year, as well as OEM inventory drawdowns and a weaker PC market.

Microsoft launched Intel- and Qualcomm-powered Surface Pro 12 and Surface Laptop 8 devices just before its new financial year began, and the company even refreshed its smaller Surface Pro and Surface Laptop with Qualcomm’s X2 Plus chips during the quarter. Surface prices are higher than ever thanks to RAMageddon, and the PC market looks like it’s going to go through a very tough time during Microsoft’s 2027 financial year and beyond.

Microsoft CFO Amy Hood said on the company’s earnings call that she expects Windows OEM revenue to decline again next quarter, around “the mid 30s,” and a decline is expected to continue throughout the 2027 financial year.Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella also remarked on the company’s new hybrid AI push for Windows during the earnings call. “We are executing on our vision to bring hybrid intelligence to every desk in every home, making every PC a secure agent box,” said Nadella. “As more capable models run locally, we see an opportunity to make more intelligence effectively unmetered.”

Microsoft and Nvidia unveiled a range of RTX Spark laptops earlier this month, including the Surface Laptop Ultra. The laptops ship with up to 128GB of unified memory, and are capable of running recent frontier models locally.

The first quarter of the 2027 financial year for Xbox has been dominated by a “reset” with thousands of layoffs and studios spun off. The layoffs aren’t over yet, with rumors more could arrive in November and January. In a memo earlier this year, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma said “we will return Xbox to player and revenue growth” by the end of fiscal year 2027 (which runs through next June).

The aim, as Sharma puts it, is to “scale what works” so that by fiscal year 2030 Xbox is “halfway to our long-term daily-player goal with sustained double-digit growth in players and engagement and industry leading margins.” Sharma posted on X that Xbox was “making progress, with a lot more work ahead.” Sharma also revealed that PC platform revenue has grown in double digits this quarter, and that “console sales remain constrained by supply, with more demand than consoles available.”

Hood says Xbox hardware revenue will decline again next quarter, but Xbox content and services revenue is expected to increase in the “mid to high single digits.” Sharma says Microsoft is “working to get more consoles into players’ hands, including through our expanded refurbishment program.”

Microsoft’s cloud revenues were really the star of the earnings show again this quarter, a phrase I seem to always write every few months. Microsoft Cloud revenue contributed $63.9 billion towards Microsoft’s revenues this quarter, almost 70 percent. That’s up 30 percent year-over-year, thanks to strong demand for Azure and Microsoft 365 commercial cloud.

Nadella also discussed his vision for Microsoft’s own AI models on the company’s earnings call. “We also continue to make rapid progress with our first-party models,” said Nadella. “Our goal is to advance the cost to outcome frontier, which means delivering capabilities comparable to leading models within three to six months of their release at a fraction of the training cost, thereby allowing us to transfer those cost benefits to customers.”

Update, October 28th: Article updated with comments from Microsoft CFO Amy Hood, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella, and Xbox CEO Asha Sharma.

#219. The FTC asks companies from Uber to Walmart about how they personalize the prices you pay

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Federal Trade Commission is asking companies from food delivery services to airlines about how they use personalized prices.

The Commission said on Wednesday that it sent orders to the companies asking for details about what kind of personal information they use to determine prices. The orders target a wide range of companies, from retailers Walmart and Target to food delivery services Uber and DoorDash to airlines like Delta and United.

"Consumers deserve to know whether companies use their web surfing habits or other personal data to estimate the highest price they can charge each consumer," said FTC Chairman Andrew N. Ferguson.

"Workers also deserve to know if their compensation is affected by how companies use their personal data," Ferguson added.

Several companies pushed back against the FTC's claims.

"There is no fare product Delta has ever used, is testing or plans to use that targets customers with individualized offers based on personal data," a Delta spokesperson said.

"We do not use personal information to set prices, and we never will," Instacart said in a statement.

Walmart and Target previously said they would be transparent about their pricing strategies as more stores start using digital price tags.

A growing number of companies are using dynamic pricing to determine what customers pay for everything from concert tickets to jeans.

Some companies use similar methods to determine how much workers get paid. Uber presents its drivers with potential payouts that aren't based on a per-mile or per-minute rate, for instance, but instead consider factors such as demand.

The FTC's orders ask the companies to submit information about their pricing methodologies, including how they use AI, going back to January 1, 2023. It also asks for research that the companies have done on their pricing strategies, such as A/B tests.

The Commission is also seeking information about how the companies used similar technology to offer workers jobs, according to the orders.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google has released its latest frontier model, Gemini 4 Carbon, offering “significant gains” in coding, agentic tasks, and cybersecurity. In an announcement on Wednesday, Google highlights Carbon’s top spot on the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index (v5.0) benchmark, with Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra trailing behind.

#240. Hacker stole personnel data about thousands of Homeland Security employees, including CBP and ICE agents

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW.

Personally identifiable information belonging to thousands of U.S. Department of Homeland Security employees has been published online after a hacker stole reams of data in a previously undisclosed breach at the government department earlier this year.

The breach includes names, job titles, and work contact information, such as email addresses, cell phone numbers, and office locations, of more than 40,000 people who work for the federal department, including border and immigration agents, cybersecurity staffers, and senior department officials.

A hacker who goes by the handle Department of Peace claimed they obtained the data from an Okta single sign-on system used by Homeland Security to allow employees and contractors across its various agencies to log in to its central network. The Okta system also contains IT and log-in records associated with the employees’ user account, including details about which systems and internal apps they are permitted to access.

More than half of the dataset — some 21,000 records — relate to employees with U.S. Customs and Border Protection (CBP), who work at airports and other ports of entry.

The data also contains records associated with another 10,000 employees of Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and its Homeland Security Investigations (HSI) unit, and just under a 1,000 employees who work at U.S. cybersecurity defense agency CISA.

Thousands of additional records relate to staff who work at the department’s headquarters and its other agencies, including U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services and the Federal Protective Service.

“They have our information, so it’s only fair that we have theirs,” said Department of Peace in a note left with the data, which TechCrunch has seen. The hacker did not respond to TechCrunch’s email requesting comment and with questions about how the breach was carried out.

This is the latest data breach to hit the U.S. government in recent months, following earlier hacks involving the theft of thousands of FBI agents’ personnel records and the personal information of millions of serving and former military service personnel from the Pentagon’s human resources unit. It’s also the second breach involving Homeland Security this year, after the same hacker claimed to have taken thousands of contracts between the department and the private sector, including defense contractors and surveillance vendors.

The latest batch of breached data, which appears authentic, offers a rare — albeit incomplete — view into the scale and operations of Homeland Security, whose deportations, arbitrary detentions, and killings of U.S. citizens under its leadership have become a key issue for voters ahead of the upcoming U.S. midterm elections.

The stolen Homeland Security data was provided to journalist and hacker maia arson crimew, who then shared a copy with DDoSecrets, a nonprofit collective that hosts leaked datasets in the public interest.

TechCrunch verified a portion of the data as authentic by matching the names of several known Homeland Security employees against an offline copy of the database. The thousands of employee directory records appear to have been exfiltrated around mid-August, according to the timestamps on each person’s record. The number of leaked records is equivalent to about one-fifth of Homeland Security’s workforce, which totals around 220,000 staff.

It’s unclear whether the vulnerability that allowed the breach has been fixed or if Homeland Security is aware of the data theft.

A spokesperson for Homeland Security told TechCrunch after publication: “DHS is aware of a potential breach and is actively working to address the situation swiftly and effectively. We take all security threats against our employees and officers seriously and will work to ensure all are properly protected. We will update the public when more information is available.”

When reached, Okta said it was aware of this report. “Okta is secure, and we have no evidence our data or systems were compromised,” a spokesperson said.

While the leaked data includes identifiable information about agents and officers, the dataset also reveals more about the surveillance technologies and cybersecurity tools that Homeland Security employees may be able to access. Critics have called for investigations into tech companies that fund or provide their technology to ICE.

The leaked data contains information about employees’ membership in user groups within the department’s Active Directory, a system that controls access to the department’s internal tools, services, and third-party offerings used for work.

Most of the Homeland Security employees in the dataset were members of the user group that had access to Microsoft 365, the tech giant’s cloud service that hosts the agency’s emails and files, as well as user groups for accessing Adobe Acrobat, Citrix, and other typical desktop software.

The data also shows that around 4,500 users, including ICE agents and deportation officers, are members of a group called “Headspace_users.” Online therapy provider Headspace previously had an $82,500 federal contract with ICE that ended in 2023 to fund “mental health software required for ERO employees,” a reference to ICE’s deportation unit.

A spokesperson for Headspace said the company has not provided any services to DHS or ICE since 2023.

Dozens of other user groups reference Penlink, a web surveillance company that makes technology it claims can track the location data of hundreds of millions of mobile devices. Penlink has had a contract with ICE for years. The user groups vary in size, from a handful of users in local Homeland Security offices around the country to more than 2,000 users across ICE’s HSI unit nationally. One group specifically references Penlink PLX, which the company says is used to capture and analyze “real-time intelligence” from phone calls, texts, web activity, and social media.

A spokesperson for Penlink did not respond to a request for comment.

One user group also includes more than 200 CBP users associated with Mobile Fortify, a facial-recognition app used by both CBP and ICE agents to identify people and verify their information and immigration status.

The user groups also include several employees associated with ChatGPT access, including senior DHS officials, as well as other groups associated with investigations into the China-backed hacking groups known as Salt Typhoon and Volt Typhoon.

Researchers and public information advocates say that the exposed data could provide valuable leads for investigations into alleged abuses by the Trump administration, such as identifying email addresses of agents that can be used in public records requests under the Freedom of Information Act.

“Email addresses and phone numbers are indispensable tools for FOIA researchers, often making the difference between a vague records request that gets stonewalled and one that gets results,” said Ryan Shapiro, the executive director of transparency nonprofit Property of the People.

“Given the Trump administration’s contempt for even basic transparency, the need for such tools is urgent,” said Shapiro.

DDoSecrets co-founder and journalist Emma Best said that the exposed Homeland Security data will be a “catalyst for investigations, empowering journalists, Congressional inquiries, legal challenges, community action and Freedom of Information Act requests.”

Updated with comment from Homeland Security.

#216. Bessent says 'not necessarily' a mistake if the Fed hikes again

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said Wednesday that further Federal Reserve rate hikes wouldn't necessarily be a mistake.

After CNBC's Brian Sullivan asked if rate increases in the future would constitute a monetary policy error, Bessent responded: "That's not necessarily so."

Bessent's comments came hours after the Fed announced it would keep interest rates unchanged at its October gathering, as the market widely expected.

Bessent is a key confidant of President Donald Trump, who has repeatedly called for lower interest rates. Trump pressured Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh's predeccesor, Jerome Powell, over the issue during Powell's final months at the helm of the central bank.

But Bessent said Wednesday that both he and Trump believe Warsh is doing well and can strike a balance between ensuring full employment and stable prices.

"I think Chair Warsh is off to a great start," Bessent said. "As I often say, I have great confidence in him."

The Fed's decision to hold rates steady came a month after it lifted borrowing costs for the first time in three years. Fed funds futures traders widely expected the central bank to return to rate hiking when it meets again in December.

Bessent said he would like to see the Fed have an "open mind" about the supply shock from energy prices having a "transitory nature." The U.S. war with Iran has led to a sharp drop in global crude production volumes, in turn leading annual inflation readings to move further from the Fed's target of 2%.

Bessent also said the U.S. efforts to isolate the Iran economy, dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, have been "very successful." He said internal estimates show that Iran's economy could contract by as much as 25% in the span of a year.

When asked about bond yields on artificial-intelligence related debt, the Wall Street veteran said "we monitor" it, but that it didn't need to cause concern.

Bessent specifically referenced neoclouds, a type of company providing specific cloud infrastructure supporting AI that has recently struggled in the market. Tesla CEO Elon Musk warned on Sunday that more than 200 neocloud firms "are going to see guppy-level death and consolidation."

"I don't think it's problematic at all," Bessent said. "The neoclouds are a small component of the overall ecosystem, and we're still seeing the financing getting done. We're still seeing the deals get done."

Bessent told CNBC he expects the third-quarter GDP data expected Thursday to be "strong." But he clarified that "I haven't seen it yet."

#396. Massive Leak Exposes 41,000 Homeland Security Workers—Including Undercover ICE Agents

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An unprecedented leak of Department of Homeland Security records has exposed details of more than 41,000 employees or contractors who work for the department and its constituent agencies. The records include email addresses, phone numbers, and office addresses, but also references to internal systems and sensitive information on undercover and surveillance programs. In all, the leak provides a map of how DHS is organized and how it operates.

The records, which span DHS itself as well as seven component agencies, including Immigration and Customs Enforcement and US Customs and Border Protection, were last updated on August 19, 2026. The transparency nonprofit Distributed Denial of Secrets and cybersecurity researcher and journalist maia arson crimew previewed the data for WIRED, which they say they obtained from a group calling itself the Department of Peace.

DDoSecrets published the archive, which it calls “Icepick,” on Wednesday. In a press release, DDoSecrets says the data is from DHS's Okta login system. In a note with the files, the hackers wrote, “They have our information, so it’s only fair that we have theirs.”

An Okta spokesperson tells WIRED that it is unaware of any breach of its systems. “We are aware of this report. Okta is secure, and we have no evidence our data or systems were compromised,” the spokesperson says.

The leak comes as DHS has aggressively pursued its critics, seeking to unmask anonymous social media users, obtain customer records on protesters, and secure warrants for surveillance tools aimed at reporters. Since the start of the second Trump administration, DHS and the federal government have moved to hide DHS employees’ names and faces. The agency has also expanded its definition of “doxing”—a term that typically refers to publishing someone’s home address and other personal details for malicious purposes—to encompass the publication of basic facts about public employees, like their names and titles.

The leak follows a breach in which a group of hackers known as ShinyHunters claimed to have stolen personal information on nearly every FBI employee and their families. Law enforcement agencies have arrested several suspects and detained another who is alleged to be part of the group.

The DHS records are searchable on a website crimew built. “I don't think we should let them scare us into not looking into them,” she tells WIRED.

“DHS is aware of a potential breach and is actively working to address the situation swiftly and effectively,” a Homeland Security spokesperson tells WIRED. “We take all security threats against our employees and officers seriously and will work to ensure all are properly protected. We will update the public when more information is available.”

The DOJ did not respond to WIRED’s request for comment. The FBI referred WIRED’s inquiry to DHS.

According to a WIRED analysis of the data, CBP staffers account for more than half of the records. ICE accounts for about 10,000, roughly a third of them in Homeland Security Investigations, ICE’s investigative wing. Another 6,200 belong to US Citizenship and Immigration Services, and nearly 1,000 are tied to the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, more than half of them contractors. Altogether, the files cover about a seventh of DHS’s workforce, revealing how DHS is structured, from senior leadership down to individual immigration agents and detention contractors.

Like most large organizations, DHS uses permission groups to control access to internal systems, according to the leaked records. Nearly everyone named in the leak has access to at least some of these systems. At HSI, 39 employees across more than a dozen field offices belong to groups whose names include “CUC,” which government court filings say stands for “certified undercover operation.” Another group, apparently based in HSI's St. Paul, Minnesota, area of operations, references "Keyhole," which federal court filings describe as a certified undercover operation that recently funded HSI’s undercover surveillance of Minneapolis protest groups earlier this year. Others reference a “Communications Intercept Center TechOps (CICTO) Program,” a “Title III Intercept Program” for wiretaps, and an “Illicit Sensor Intercept Program (ISIP).”

The names of the groups—there are over 55,000 in all—appear to reference tools for accessing immigration court records, pulling data from seized phones, and conducting surveillance. These include Palantir’s Falcon investigative platform and PenLink, which analyzes phone records and social media.

Two permission groups, named “Executive Leadership” and “AI Access,” appear to list people given exceptions to DHS's web filter, which blocks some websites for employees. They contain the same 13 people, including former DHS head Kristi Noem; Antoine McCord, the recently departed DHS chief information officer who has returned to defense tech company Anduril; Philip Hegseth, listed as a senior adviser to the DHS secretary and the younger brother of defense secretary Pete Hegseth; and Jayce Krachenfels, listed as a policy adviser, who previously worked as a regional field director for Donald Trump’s 2024 presidential campaign, according to his LinkedIn profile. Noem, McCord, Hegseth, and Krachenfels did not respond to a request for comment

Other groups named in the leak reference AI tools, including OpenAI’s ChatGPT, Google’s Gemini, and Microsoft’s Copilot. One, called “Grok.com,” includes eight DHS technology officials, including the department’s former chief information officer and chief data officer.

“If authentic and current, this is an extraordinarily valuable intelligence resource for those seeking to penetrate DHS,” says Alexander Leslie, a national security expert at the threat intelligence company Recorded Future. “This dataset becomes way more valuable when correlated with previously compromised information, commercial data, and other intelligence collections. An adversary could spend months exploiting these relationships to identify vulnerable personnel, develop convincing impersonation attempts, or compromise ongoing investigations.”

More than 700 accounts are associated with a group apparently referencing Signal, the encrypted messaging app, nearly all belonging to CBP personnel. More than 200 CBP accounts belong to a group referencing Mobile Fortify, a face-recognition app that immigration agents use to identify people. The group includes field supervisors, special response team operators, and tactical and intelligence agents. About half are in Texas, California, and Arizona.

Roughly 1,300 ICE enforcement accounts have been created since July 4, 2025, when Trump signed the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, which funded the hiring of 10,000 new ICE Enforcement and Removal officers by the end of 2025.

Nearly 20 accounts appear to belong to personnel with the Department of Defense, which the administration styles as the Department of War; eight are in a group titled “ICE Tip Line DOW Surge.” About 170 accounts are listed as providing “military processing support” at ICE facilities, and 35 others explicitly identify National Guard members.

Dozens of accounts have job titles like “Designated Immigration Officer” and “287(g) Officer,” which mark local law enforcement personnel authorized to carry out some immigration enforcement duties. Many list county sheriff's offices and jails as their workplaces. The records also include roughly 4,400 “associates,” whose jobs range from IT developers and data scientists to nurses and detention officers. Of those, 145 are named as a “contractor” or “high risk contractor.”

More than 300 records reference senior titles associated with DHS headquarters, from Noem down through deputy undersecretaries, assistant secretaries, and chiefs of staff to officials overseeing intelligence, technology, biometrics, and privacy. Most records also name a supervisor, which could allow anyone analyzing the dataset to reconstruct reporting relationships across the department.

The Trump administration has prosecuted protesters for posting information about individual ICE officers and warned others for doing so. In June, 15 Minnesota residents were charged under laws related to stalking and threats of violence, which prosecutors linked to their participation in ICE-opposition activities. More than 100 HSI agents, including undercover operatives, took part in an operation that included widespread surveillance and infiltration of group chats.

Adam Steinbaugh, a senior attorney at the nonprofit civil liberties group FIRE, says that the Trump administration has taken an expansive view of 18 USC 119, a law that prohibits people from sharing “restricted personal information” about federal officers or their families with the intent to threaten them.

“This is information that would be on a business card,” says Steinbaugh of the names and basic work details included in the archive. “That’s not something the government can criminalize."

Despite the potential sensitivity of other records in the leaked data, the creators of Icepick do not consider it doxing. They see it as an accountability tool.

“The DHS especially as an agency completely lacks accountability, despite many parts of it being incredibly unpopular. The people who work there know this too; for example, some of their LinkedIn bios simply say ‘stuff at US Government,’” says crimew. “If they weren't hiding their involvement in the repressive arm of the US government, there would be no need for us to expose them in the first place.”

Updated 10/28/2026 at 8:02 pm ET: Added comment from DHS.
