#180. Do you actually need an electrolyte powder? Here Is what to know before you buy

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NBC Selected independently determines what we cover and recommend. When you click on or buy through our links, we earn a commission. Learn more.

Drinking plenty of water is essential to staying hydrated, but sometimes, H20 alone doesn’t cut it, says Dr. Matthew Sedgley, a physician specializing in sports medicine at MedStar Health. Those who do strenuous exercise, have a labor-intensive job or spend long periods in the heat may also need to replenish their electrolytes. “Electrolyte is a fancy word for minerals like sodium, potassium and magnesium, and they’re substances our bodies need to function,” he says. “Your body uses them as you do physical activity, which makes replacing them important.”

Mixing electrolyte powder into your water is a fast and convenient way to do that, says Amy Goldsmith, a registered dietitian specializing in sports nutrition who owns Kindred Nutrition & Kinetics. These powders often come in pre-measured packets, so you know exactly how much of each mineral you’re getting, and they’re available in various flavors, so they taste good.

I talked to doctors and nutritionists about how to shop for electrolyte powders, as well as who should (and shouldn’t) use them. But remember: Before you take anything, be sure to consult your doctor.

Want more from NBC Selected? Sign up for our newsletter, The Selection, and shop smarter.

Before buying electrolyte powder, it’s helpful to understand what it is, what benefits it offers and who it’s designed for. Here’s some key information experts want you to know.

Electrolytes are minerals like sodium, potassium and calcium that help fluids enter and exit your cells to keep you hydrated, says Jay Patruno, a registered dietitian and certified personal trainer. (They’re called electrolytes because they have an electrical charge when dissolved in water or bodily fluids.) These minerals also play an important role in muscle and nerve contraction, creating energy and building tissue, says Michael Reavis Jr., a clinical dietitian at the University of Maryland Medical Center.

Your body doesn’t make electrolytes: You consume them through food. All foods have electrolytes, but some are richer in specific minerals than others, says Patruno. For example, salt is rich in sodium and chloride, bananas are rich in potassium, yogurt is rich in calcium and nuts are rich in magnesium, says Reavis. You mainly lose electrolytes through sweat, though your kidneys also filter out excess electrolytes in urine, says Lisa Young, a registered nutritionist and an adjunct professor of nutrition at New York University.

Everyone loses electrolytes at different volumes depending on overall health status, level of physical activity and sweat rate, meaning how many ounces of fluid you lose per hour while sweating, says Goldsmith. Your doctor can evaluate these things to determine how you should go about replacing electrolytes.

Electrolyte powder is a mixture of minerals like sodium, potassium and magnesium that you add to water to make a fortified drink. It’s a type of electrolyte product, like chews, drops and gels. Since you dissolve electrolyte powder in water, they help balance your body’s fluid and electrolyte levels simultaneously, which is key to staying hydrated, says Young.

Electrolyte powders are designed to replace electrolytes you lose, mainly through sweat, and support fast rehydration so you avoid symptoms of depletion, like fatigue, blood pressure irregularities and lightheadedness, says Kelly Jones, a sports dietitian who works with professional and student-athletes. “They help you get a significant amount of electrolytes in a very short amount of time, give you all the different types of minerals you need at once, and you know exactly how much of each one you’re consuming,” says Goldsmith. “There’s not really a food or drink that can naturally do that.”

It’s important to note that while electrolyte products like powders can help you stay hydrated, they’re not a replacement for plain water, says Sedgley. Electrolyte products should be used as needed in specific situations like intense exercise, while water is essential to drink throughout the day to stay hydrated and keep up your fluid levels, he says.

The most important electrolytes to replace are sodium, which you lose at the highest rate while sweating, and potassium, which comes in second, says Jones. Electrolytes like calcium, magnesium, chloride and phosphorus are also important for hydration, but if you exercise for less than two hours at a time, the amount you lose is negligible, says Jones. So focus on getting these nutrients through food, she says.

Electrolyte powders mainly benefit the average person who engages in an hour or more of moderate to intense exercise, says Sedgley. Moderate exercise involves activities like brisk walking or light jogging, where talking is a bit more challenging than usual, and intense exercise involves activities like fast running, cycling or high-intensity interval training, where talking is difficult, he says.

Electrolyte powders also support recovery and hydration for people who do physical activity in the heat, says Sedgley. That includes runners, bikers, those playing in sporting events, construction workers and landscapers, for example. But in hot conditions, even low-intensity exercise like walking may leave you feeling depleted, he says. People who spend all day on their feet, like teachers and medical professionals, can also benefit from electrolyte powders, says Jones.

Below, I rounded up electrolyte powders I’ve tried or experts recommend. I included the amount of sodium and potassium each one has per serving since they’re the most important minerals to look for while shopping, experts say.

When adding powder to water, don't forget to give it a good stir — if you don’t, the powder won’t fully dissolve into your water, and you may end up sipping salty clumps. Try using a shaker bottle or a handheld electric mixer to thoroughly incorporate the powder into water if needed.

Goldsmith recommends Body Armor’s electrolyte powders over the brand’s pre-mixed beverages for hydration because they have less sugar and more sodium: One packet of the powder has 510 milligrams of sodium and zero grams of sugar, while one pre-mixed beverage has 30 milligrams of sodium and 29 grams of sugar. I’m also a big fan of this electrolyte powder, which the brand sent me to try. Its flavor isn’t overpowering or too sweet, and beyond sodium and potassium, it’s made with chloride, calcium and magnesium. I love the tropical punch flavor, and it’s available in options like lemon-lime and strawberry.

“Some brands offer electrolyte tablets rather than powders, which tend to be less messy,” says Jones. “The tabs dissolve in water and usually come in a small tube that’s easy to keep on hand while you’re on the go.” In my experience, electrolyte tablets take a bit longer than powders because you can’t mix them — you have to wait until the tablet completely dissolves on its own before drinking.

I’ve used Nuun’s electrolyte tablets for years. They make your water fizzy when they dissolve, which I find is more enjoyable to drink. The tablets have sodium, potassium, magnesium, calcium and chloride, plus they’re gluten-free and vegan. You can buy Nuun’s electrolyte tablets in flavors like strawberry lemonade (my favorite), watermelon and tri–berry. They’re also eligible to buy with a flexible spending account (FSA).

Liquid IV, which Goldsmith recommends, is one of the most well-known brands in the electrolyte powder space. It comes in over a dozen flavors like lemon lime, strawberry and concord grape, so you’ll likely find one you like. Each pack has 11 grams of sugar, which I find gives it a very sweet taste, so I tend to dilute it with a few more ounces of water than the brand recommends. Liquid IV’s sodium- and potassium-based electrolyte powder is vegan, dairy-free and gluten-free.

DripDrop’s variety of flavors is similar to Liquid IV’s, but the formula has lower sodium and sugar concentrations.

That gives it a less pronounced taste, so I find it ideal for those who’ve never tried an electrolyte powder before and are getting used to drinking it. The powder is made with sodium, potassium and magnesium, and its formula is gluten-free and vegan. You can buy flavors like fruit punch, orange, lemon-lime and berry.

This electrolyte powder’s formula has a 2:1 ratio of sodium to potassium, which Goldsmith aims for when choosing a well-balanced product for her clients. It’s also third-party tested and Informed Choice Certified for purity and safety. The powder is made with calcium and magnesium and is free from artificial sweeteners, artificial coloring, dyes and gluten. You can buy flavors like raspberry lemonade and tropical punch.

In addition to single-serve packets, Transparent Labs’ Hydrate electrolyte powder comes in a jar with a scooper. Make sure you level off the scoop before mixing with water, says Reavis. If you don’t, the electrolyte concentration may be slightly greater than what’s listed on the product’s nutrition label.

Because I find Liquid IV’s standard electrolyte powder very sweet, I prefer the brand’s sugar-free sodium- and potassium-based formula.

Instead of cane sugar or artificial sweeteners, it’s made with allulose, a natural sugar found in fruit like figs and raisins. My favorite flavors are lemon-lime and white peach, which taste especially refreshing in ice water. Liquid IV’s sugar-free, vegan, dairy-free and gluten-free electrolyte powder also comes in flavors like raspberry melon, green grape and mandarin orange.

Gatorade’s electrolyte powder, which many experts recommend, has an ingredient profile that’s similar to its Gatorlyte sports drink.

The powder is a great option if you like the taste of Gatorade but want the convenience of a single-serve packet you can throw in your gym bag. You can buy the powder, made with sodium, potassium, calcium, magnesium and chloride, in flavors like cherry lime, orange and watermelon.

If you want a slightly sweet taste and fewer added carbohydrates than a sports drink, “Thorne Catalyte is a great option, and it’s also NSF Certified for Sport,” says Jones. “With 485 milligrams of sodium and five grams of carbohydrate, it’s perfect for active individuals.” Young also recommends this lemon-lime electrolyte powder, made with sodium, potassium, calcium, magnesium and chloride. Thorne Catalyte has no dairy, sugar, gluten or artificial flavors.

Since it has a moderate sodium concentration of 400 milligrams, Jones recommends Skratch Labs’ Hydration powder for those who have active careers, like construction workers, nurses, teachers and landscapers, and those who exercise for under an hour but tend to sweat a lot while doing so. The vegan powder is made with sodium, potassium, calcium and magnesium, and it’s gluten- and dairy-free. Skratch Labs’ electrolyte powder comes in flavors like pineapple, strawberry lemonade and orange.

“For those with very high sweat rates, I often suggest incorporating LMNT to aid in rehydration and maintaining energy levels during exercise,” says Jones.

LMNT’s vegan, gluten-free electrolyte powder has the highest sodium concentration out of all the options on this list at 1,000 milligrams, making it suitable for those engaged in prolonged intense physical activity, like marathon runners and long-distance bikers. Its formula has magnesium as well as sodium and potassium. You can buy the powder in flavors like citrus salt, watermelon salt, raspberry salt and mango chili salt.

Electrolyte replacement products that help prevent dehydration and don’t contain added supplements are eligible for reimbursement with an FSA, a health savings account (HSA) or a health reimbursement arrangement (HRA).

Very few brands currently qualify, but Cure is one of them. Its 240 milligrams of sodium is a low concentration, which makes it a great option for first-timers. Cure sent me flavors likeberry pomegranate, watermelon, lemonade and strawberry kiwi to try, all of which tasted great and dissolved quickly when I stirred the powder into water.

I often crave an intensely flavored beverage during workouts, but I can’t always stomach something super sweet or salty before I exercise.

That’s when I use Moon Juice’s electrolyte powder, which the brand sent me to try. It helps me preload electrolytes before a long run or hot yoga class, but I barely taste the powder. Moon Juice’s Mini Dew electrolyte powder is sugar-free and made with sodium, potassium, calcium, magnesium and chloride. Moon Juice also offers a pomelo-flavored version.

You lose electrolytes while you’re sick if you’re vomiting or having diarrhea, says Mallipattu.

In those cases, he recommends drinking Pedialyte, available in powders or as a pre-mixed liquid solution. It has a very simple formula mainly composed of sodium, potassium and chloride, which is similar to what you’d get through IV fluids at the hospital, he says.

While shopping for electrolyte powders, experts recommend keeping the following in mind, all of which I took into consideration while curating a list of the best options.

The FDA sets Daily Value recommendations for amounts of certain nutrients you should not exceed each day, including electrolytes like sodium, potassium and calcium. However, these recommendations don’t account for how much of each mineral you lose and need to replace while doing physical activity, says Goldsmith. Use the FDA’s Daily Value recommendations as a guide while evaluating electrolyte powders’ nutrition labels, though these are generally based on 2,000 calories per day, which might be too high or too low for some people, says Patruno. Talk to a registered dietitian or your doctor about what’s right for you.

Sodium is the most important mineral to prioritize when buying an electrolyte powder since you lose it at the highest rate while sweating, says Jones. However, how much sodium you need depends on your activity level, sweat rate and overall health status. Below, experts share their recommendations for sodium concentration in electrolyte powder, but consult your doctor to determine what’s best for you.

After sodium, pay attention to potassium since it’s the mineral you lose at the second highest rate through sweat, says Jones. Many electrolyte powders have a 2:1 ratio of sodium to potassium, like 500 milligrams of sodium to 250 milligrams of potassium, for example. High sodium intake with insufficient potassium can quickly increase blood pressure, but a balanced 2:1 ratio helps prevent that from happening as the electrolytes move through the cells, says Sedgley.

Some electrolyte powders contain additional minerals like calcium, chloride, magnesium and phosphorus. They’re less important from a hydration standpoint, so don’t worry about them unless your doctor advises you to, says Jones.

When you do over an hour of intense exercise like running or biking, your body uses sugar, a carbohydrate, as fuel for your muscles, says Goldsmith. Sugar also helps with fluid balance and masks the salty taste of electrolytes, she says. Because of this, many electrolyte powders have 10 to 20 grams of added sugar per serving. “In terms of athletic performance, we think of sugar in an electrolyte powder differently than added sugar in regular food,” says Goldsmith. “This amount of added sugar per serving could be considered excessive if you’re sedentary and eat a lot of foods with added sugars, but from an exercise perspective, it’s necessary for your muscles.” That said, it’s important to think about your daily sugar intake to properly assess your needs, so consult your doctor.

Electrolyte powders are made with different types of sugar, including natural sugars like honey and agave, cane sugar and artificial sweeteners like stevia and xylitol. Each type of sugar has a different flavor and glycemic index, meaning how quickly it raises blood sugar levels after you eat it. The average healthy person doesn’t have to think much about the type of sugar in an electrolyte powder and can prioritize their taste preferences, says Young. But if you have a medical condition like diabetes and need to be careful about sugar consumption, consult your doctor about what’s best for your body. Young also says that artificial sweeteners tend to give some people an upset stomach, so consider avoiding them if you have a history of gastrointestinal issues.

There’s no perfect time to drink electrolyte powder. Everybody is different, so figuring out what’s right for you involves some trial and error, as well as consulting your doctor.

Overall, “hydration is a lot like pain management,” says Goldsmith, “Once you’re behind, it’s really hard to get ahead of it.” That said, ensure you’re drinking enough water daily, regardless of whether you’re adding electrolyte powder.

Suppose you don’t engage in much physical activity during your day-to-day life and aren’t heavily sweating. In that case, you can likely get plenty of electrolytes from a healthy, balanced diet, says Reavis, so electrolyte powder is unnecessary. However, some people find that adding a small amount of flavored electrolyte powder to their water — like half a packet or less — encourages them to drink. So, as long as you consult your doctor and choose an electrolyte powder with a low amount of sodium (200 mg to 300 mg.), do what works for you, says Reavis.

Additionally, a few groups of people need to be extra cautious when it comes to using electrolyte powder, making it even more important to work closely with a doctor to find the right option:

An electrolyte imbalance occurs when there’s either too many or insufficient electrolytes in your body, says Sedgley. Some symptoms of an electrolyte imbalance overlap with those of dehydration, and you can experience both conditions simultaneously — overall, however, they’re different from one another. Dehydration has to do with water loss, while an electrolyte imbalance has to do with being deficient or abundant in certain minerals, says Mallipattu. (If you’re concerned about experiencing dehydration and/or an electrolyte imbalance, consult your doctor.)

An electrolyte imbalance can present differently depending on the minerals your body has too much or too little of, says Mallipattu. If you’re low or high in sodium, for example, you may experience symptoms like confusion or an unstable gait, he says. If you’re low in potassium, you may experience muscle weakness and cramps or, in more severe cases, heart arrhythmias (irregular heartbeat), says Mallipattu.

Hyponatremia, a condition that occurs when there’s not enough sodium in your blood, is one of the main types of electrolyte depletion Sedgley sees during marathons and similar physically demanding events. “If someone stopped at every water station on the course and they’ve drunk too much, plus they’re sweating out salt, they may have diluted the sodium in their blood,” he says, noting that symptoms of hyponatremia include muscle cramps, nausea, headaches, confusion and fatigue.

Beyond electrolyte powder, brands make electrolyte drops, chews, gels and pre-mixed drinks like Gatorade and Powerade. Each product offers its own set of benefits, but for the most part, they’re variations on the same theme, so choosing one is a matter of personal preference. However, keep in mind that electrolyte products can differ in their average mineral concentration, which impacts how much one serving can help boost your hydration levels.

“You can’t get as high of a concentration in one serving of an electrolyte drop or chew as you can get in a powder,” says Goldsmith. “That means you usually have to use more of those other products more often.”

Some people also use different electrolyte products depending on the situation. Chews or gels, for instance, are typically easier for runners to fit in their fanny packs and quickly take during a race. But those who play sports like baseball or soccer can keep a water bottle on the sidelines and mix in powders.

Yes, to some degree, drinking electrolyte powder can help with a hangover, says Mallipattu. “You often end up urinating a lot if you drink a lot of alcohol, so you lose water and some sodium,” he says. “Therefore the electrolytes do make a difference, but eating a salty meal may help as well.”

Experts recommended some of their favorite options, and I also included products I’ve tried.

#235. What smart people are saying after the Fed held rates steady

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Federal Reserve held interest rates steady as was widely expected. The rate remained unchanged after a 10-2 vote, where 2 officials voted to hike rates by 25 bps.

Fed chair Kevin Warsh, who began his term leading the central bank in May, said the Fed is working to deliver future price stability and quash inflation during the Federal Open Market Committee press conference following the hold announcement.

"The period right in front of us is information rich," Warsh said.

He added: "We're going to be able to figure out what's the appropriate protocol, as part of this package that we started to deliver five months ago, to deliver price stability."

In September, the Fed opted for a quarter-point increase — the first hike in three years — aimed at tackling rising inflation. The decision to hold rates comes less than a week before US midterm elections, where the country's affordability crisis and rising energy and oil prices are fresh on voters' minds. Warsh said politics played no role in the decision.

Here is how smart people in business and finance are reacting to the hold — and why many expect another rate hike in December.

Haigh is among the Fed watchers who expect a hike to be the likely outcome of the final Fed meeting of the year.

"We expect the Fed to resume rate hikes in December," Haigh said. "September's strong CPI print is a reminder that upside inflation risks remain, and the Fed is likely to look to take out further insurance."

Rick said Wednesday's decision to keep rates steady made sense after September's first hike since July 2023.

Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.

"Monetary policy works with long and variable lags, so the Fed will want to see how the higher rates already in the system are affecting consumer spending, business investment and hiring before determining whether additional tightening is necessary," he said. "There's no need to rush."

Davis said the Fed's wait-and-see approach makes sense as inflation remains above the Fed's 2% target and the job market has cooled without "showing the kind of widespread deterioration we'd typically associate with a recession."

"One thing worth watching is how the bond market responds," Davis said. "The Fed controls short-term interest rates, but longer-term yields reflect a much broader set of forces, including inflation expectations, economic growth and Treasury supply. With the 10-year Treasury yield around 5%, financial conditions have already tightened considerably. Higher borrowing costs can do some of the Fed's work without another rate hike."

"The Fed's choice to hold today will have little noticeable impact for everyday household life," she said. "But consumers paying attention could feel let down by the seeming inaction on inflation from the Central Bank."

Renter suggests that families feeling financial strain cut back on discretionary expenses, as prices are likely to remain high. Those not feeling as much strain could put some money into extra savings.

Boockvar said markets would likely be on the lookout for unofficial guidance on rates from central bankers in the days and weeks ahead. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh has leaned away from the central bank issuing official formal guidance. But other Fed officials have been more forthcoming about their views on monetary policy lately, such as by speaking about rates in public remarks.

"I'm sure again Chair Warsh will dance around making any commitment and we'll be more reliant on the upcoming speeches and comments that his colleagues give," Boockvar wrote in a note to clients on Substack.

Seema Shah said the hold was a good call. Amid global and economic uncertainty, the Fed has time to gather more data. Yet, she said hikes in December and early 2027 are expected.

"AI may be booming, but the broader economy is not firing on all cylinders, and elevated geopolitical uncertainty argues against rushing another hike," she said. "But the direction of travel remains clear. The Fed is still taking back some of last year's rate cuts, and the two dissents underline that tightening bias."

Brian Coulton said the Fed is holding steady for more information before "pulling the trigger again." He also expects another hike in December, based on economic factors indicating "upside risks to the US inflation outlook."

"With 2 of the 12 voting members opting for a hike, and a more bullish assessment of current growth trends, it looks like we will get another hike in December," Coulton said. "The statement characterized economic activity as expanding at a 'strong pace' rather than the 'solid pace' description used in September."

#296. Meta weighs delaying 'alarming' new business terms as backlash grows

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company says it won't make "policy changes" until it consults with publishers.

As Meta faces an escalating backlash over its new terms for businesses, the company says it might push back their implementation as it plans "a full consultation" with publishers. But the company still hasn't changed the language of the terms, which gives the company the authority to scrape the websites of businesses that use Facebook.

The controversial new terms were originally supposed to go into effect October 30, but Meta later delayed it to November 20 after an outcry. Now, Meta spokesperson Andy Stone says that the actual implementation could be pushed back yet again, though Meta still lists November 20 on its website. "We care deeply about our partners and do not plan to make policy changes without a full consultation with industry groups and leaders," Stone said in a post on X. "We're committed to getting this right, and as we expect this consultation could extend beyond November 20, in which case we'd delay implementation of the updated terms. Until then our existing terms remain in place."

We've heard publishers' feedback on our proposed ads and business terms update. We care deeply about our partners and do not plan to make policy changes without a full consultation with industry groups and leaders.

We're committed to getting this right, and as we expect this... https://t.co/t91Q1QU8mu

— Andy Stone (@andymstone) October 28, 2026

Stone's statement comes as Meta has faced an intensifying backlash against the update, which gives it the right to scrape the websites of companies that use Facebook. The new terms state that Meta has the authority to "use automated software (such as web scrapers, bots, robots, spiders, crawlers, and user agents)" to access businesses websites "in order to provide, develop, improve, research, and maintain advertising and business products."

Publishers have raised significant concerns, with one industry group describing the change as "outrageous and probably illegal." A group of Norway-based outlets have also filed a lawsuit against the company over the new terms. This week, California Senator Adam Schiff became the second lawmaker to call on Meta to walk back the changes.

But while Meta is acknowledging the backlash, the company hasn't made any substantive changes to its terms since it removed the word "train" and added a reference to "advertising and business products" earlier this month. Stone has said that the new terms are "not about scraping news sites" and that the company will respect content owners' robots.txt preferences.

But, as legal experts and publishing groups have pointed out, robots.txt preferences are not legally binding. And AI companies regularly ignore these instructions. Meta also makes no reference to robots.txt in its terms, as Schiff points out in his letter. "Without the insertion of specific language codifying Meta's policy to respect robots.txt signals into the latest contractual terms, users lack any reliable means by which to protect their content," the senator wrote.

Meta didn't immediately respond to questions about whether it was planning further changes to its terms.

#304. The PlayStation 5 just might be Sony's final Blu-Ray player

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sony has long been a pioneer of physical media, but that could be coming to an end.

It's ironic coming from a company that has such a rich history of excellent CD, VHS and DVD players, but it looks like Sony is now committed to getting out of the disc business. With recent news of the firm discontinuing its sole remaining dedicated 4K Blu-player in its native Japan, and PS5 ditching game disc production from January 2028, the future looks bleak for Sony fans who love physical media.

Thanks to pesky disc rot, your Blu-ray collection won't last forever, and now it's possible the entire Blu-ray market could be at risk going forward. With the discontinuation of Sony's last dedicated UHD player, the writing may be on the wall for Blu-ray as a continuing format. Will the PS5 really be Sony's last remaining device capable of playing 4K movie discs? Will the PS6 even have a disc drive?

Sony officially confirmed it was discontinuing its last 4K Blu-ray player on Oct 22, 2026. The Sony UBP-X700/K, which was released in spring 2025, was the company's final standalone UHD player on the market. Though the UBP-700/K will no longer be sold in Japan, at time of writing it appears to still be available from retailers elsewhere, like Grooves Land and Walmart.

The news was first released by Japanese Sony-affiliated blog Kunkoko (with additional Western reporting by FlatpanelsHD). As a translated version of the blog post read: "Sony ends shipments of Ultra HD Blu-ray player UBP-X700/K. Following the end of Blu-ray recorder sales and the announcement that PlayStation game disc production will cease, the PS5 will be Sony's only UHD Blu-ray player in Japan."

As it noted, the standard disc-equipped PS5 (as well as the Slim or Pro PS5 with the disc drive add-on) plays both 4K and 1080p Blu-ray discs. However, the PS5 can't handle Dolby Vision in 4K movies that support the format, so Sony's current-gen console isn't as accomplished a player as the UBP-X700K.

4K Blu-ray always beats 4K streaming for picture quality, yet dwindling sales of both Blu-ray players and discs show that folks care more about easy streaming access than they do the quality of Blu-ray bitrates. For hardcore audio/visual dweebs like me, it'll be a sad day when 4K movie discs are no longer manufactured. Yet from a sales standpoint, I can reluctantly see where Sony is coming from.

Back in July, Sony confirmed that beginning January 2028, physical PS5 disc production will be halted for new PS5 titles. Moving forward, you'll only be able to buy new releases digitally from the PlayStation Store or from retailers via redemption codes. Existing discs will continue to work, but games released after this cutoff won't see a physical release.

At the time, we lamented how PlayStation had struck a massive blow to game preservation. Now the company also appears to have landed a haymaker to the hearts of film lovers who cherish owning a physical copy of their favorite movies.

This is pure speculation from someone with a large collection of both standard and 4K Blu-rays, but the killing of Sony's Blu-ray line could spell an end for the format in the not-too distant future. LG stopped production of its Blu-ray players in December 2024, while Samsung pulled out of the market way back in 2019. That leaves Panasonic as the last major manufacturer that hasn't abandoned Blu-ray (though small enthusiast brands like Magnetar & Krovatar continue to produce players). With the vast majority of big firms now out of the Blu-ray market, it's hard to envisage a long-term future for UHD discs.

Right now, it's hard to say. But it's difficult to be optimistic for a future where next-gen PlayStation consoles still sport disc drives. With the PS5 ditching game discs from early 2028, the sales incentive for Sony to incorporate an optical drive into the PS6 doesn't make much sense.

That said, an exclusive report from Insider Gaming claims the company will launch the PlayStation 6 with a detachable disc drive. According to the site's anonymous sources, "customers will be able to buy the console individually as a 'digital console', bundled with the PlayStation 6 as a 'traditional console', or upgrade later by purchasing the detachable disc drive separately."

To be clear, such PS6 disc drive speculation isn't being widely reported anywhere else, so it's easy to be skeptical. And while some of that wording is a bit confusing, the notion of a detachable drive would follow the PS5 playbook. Sony first gave gamers the option of buying an attachable optical drive when the PS5 Slim launched on October 10, 2023, selling the add-on for $79. Skip forward to November 7, 2024 when the PS5 Pro hit the market, and the supercharged console also supported the same optical drive.

Viewed through that lens, it's definitely possible Sony could launch a PS6 Digital Edition with an optional drive. From a manufacturing perspective, producing one console that supports an attachable disc drive makes more sense than separate disc and discless versions of the same machine. It could also technically be feasible for the PS6 to support the existing PS5 drive, assuming no advancements in disc tech happen by then.

Even if past leaks have claimed that the PS6 could triple the performance of the PS5, don't go betting the farm on the PlayStation 6 having a disc drive. Sony sadly seems committed to an all-digital future, and as an ardent fan of both game discs and 4K Blu-rays, that makes me mighty sad.

#331. Foreign gadget bans: all the news about the FCC’s clampdown on Chinese tech

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

What do drones, routers, and Roombas have in common? The US government wants to keep Chinese ones from entering the country. They’re the first consumer gadgets that are facing de facto import bans because of the US government’s xenophobic stance — and they might not be the last.

Originally, the US government was just worried about US networks. It’s been distrustful of Chinese telecom equipment providers Huawei and ZTE for well over a decade, eventually requiring telecom providers to “rip and replace” their gear. (Many telecoms have still failed to do so.) It established an “Entity List” for companies whose gear should be banned from import, marketing, and sale.

But under the second Trump administration, that list has been weaponized against entire home consumer gadget categories too — starting in December 2025 when Trump and Brendan Carr’s FCC banned all future foreign drones unless their companies obtained a waiver.

Next was a ban on all future foreign routers, all future foreign robots and power inverters, and within the robot ban, all future foreign robovacs. In each case, the government claims it’s because those gadgets fundamentally pose an unacceptable threat to US national security. But in each case, the US has provided zero public evidence of a threat, has done nothing about existing drones and routers and robots that might pose such a threat, and hasn’t given us any reason to trust that its waivers have anything to do with security.

Besides, there are rarely any US companies to fill the gaps left by foreign ones. Consumer gadgets are largely made overseas, including practically all routers and practically all drones — just like how your Apple, Dell, and HP laptops are not made in the US.

In this StoryStream, we’re following the latest on the foreign gadget bans.

As PCMag notes, Taiwan’s MSI has gotten conditional approval to bring two specific routers into the United States, pending certification: the Radix GRBE94 and GRBE34, which’ll likely have friendlier names when they go on sale. Netgear and Asus inexplicably got blanket approvals for all new routers earlier this year, while TP-Link is fighting for its Wi-Fi 8 ones.

TP-Link, one of the world’s leading manufacturers of consumer Wi-Fi routers, is in a precarious position in the US. The company just launched its first Wi-Fi 8 routers, but can’t bring them here because of President Donald Trump’s foreign router ban. Even though competitors Netgear and Asus make their routers in Asia, they’ve mysteriously obtained blanket waivers for all their new gear; perhaps because of alleged ties to China, TP-Link hasn’t managed to get such a waiver yet.

But TP-Link is willing to do something those companies weren’t — to invest nearly $1 billion to make routers in the United States, creating “hundreds of high-paying research and development, manufacturing, and construction jobs for American workers over the next four years,” according to a letter TP-Link sent to the FCC.

In July, we told you how the FCC — and its subcontractor TÜV Rheinland — didn’t bother to do basic Google searches before legally approving banned DJI technology for import and sale. But a new game of whack-a-drone was more successful. Public records show TÜV Rheinland asked the FCC to block applications from another “DJI front company,” Jovistar. The FCC agreed, and Jovistar is now on notice.

The FCC would like you to know that its DJI ban is justified — because of the contents of sealed letters from the Pentagon that it has now forwarded to 16 congresspeople. Do they know something they’re not saying out loud? So far, the government hasn’t revealed a single shred of public evidence about a national security threat.

“TP-Link would begin assembling these Wi-Fi 8 devices in the United States by next year,” it claims in a letter to the FCC — if the FCC will exempt its routers from the foreign router ban. Notably, both Netgear and Asus got exemptions without promising to make routers in the US, even though the FCC’s paperwork requires companies to commit to US manufacturing to get that exemption.

The FCC has been cracking down on some Chinese test labs that improperly helped authorize gadgets for import into the US — now, it wants to get rid of Chinese labs altogether starting December 2028, only authorizing labs if they have a reciprocal economic relationship with the US. It’ll vote on October 29th; you can read the whole argument here.

The US has been finding ways to stem the tide of imported Chinese gadgets, including decertifying “bad labs” that are “apparently controlled by China.” It’s now pulled credentials from two more: SLG-CPC Test Laboratory, and Shenzhen STS Test Services, “for willfully providing incorrect or false equipment test results in connection with applications for equipment authorization.”

It’s also planning to do the same to Shenzhen CTB Testing.

The Matic is our favorite robot vacuum and robo-mop, and it’s also now the first to escape the FCC’s Roomba ban.

Well, sort of — because the Matic wasn’t banned to begin with.

This spring, the United States banned all future foreign-made consumer routers — effectively all future routers — unless their makers could 1) convince the US government that they weren’t a national security threat, and 2) submit “a detailed, time-bound plan to establish or expand manufacturing in the United States.”

As of September 9th, Asus now has an exemption from the ban, just in time to bring its first early Wi-Fi 8 routers here. But Asus has not publicly announced any plan to bring manufacturing to the US. It would not even confirm to The Verge whether it had submitted a US manufacturing plan.

Remember when it was cheap and easy for me to buy DJI’s banned camera? The FCC is currently taking comments on a plan to ban online marketplaces from listing gadgets like that. Platforms would need to validate each device’s FCC status before they list it for sale. Etsy wants its “micro-entrepreneurs” to be exempt.

Dom says it’s quick and easy to snap a propeller backback onto the HoverAir Versa, and then… it can fly! Shame that HoverAir isn’t transparently telling backers that this camera has been banned in the US, and shame on the Trump administration for creating the dumb drone ban to begin with.

I am so sorry, fellow US gadget fans: the FCC’s drone ban appears to have struck again. The HoverAir Versa — a baby steadycam with snap-on propeller wings that transform it into a drone — has already stopped taking US orders just three days after its Indiegogo debut, and may be forced to abandon shipping plans in the US.

HoverAir is now telling backers it’s only shipping its camera to the US, not the piece that turns it into a drone, “due to a few remaining logistics updates with the Flight Kit.” But those “logistics” may actually be a euphemism for “we’ve been banned by the federal government.” The FCC’s database no longer has an entry for the HoverAir Versa at all, suggesting that its authorization grant has been pulled.

As a kid, I always imagined the “black market” would be like the movies: Shady vendors hawking illicit goods along dusty streets. But if you want a banned DJI Osmo Pocket 4 or Pocket 4 Pro camera in the US, you can just reach for your phone.

They’re on Temu, AliExpress, eBay, Mercari — even Amazon and Newegg — with fast, free shipping and no risk of tariffs or seizures. That’s because these cameras are already inside the United States, despite rules that ban them from entering the country.

HoverAir drones always have intriguing gimmicks. This one folds flat to fit in your pocket. This one can land on water. Another can charge inside its carrying case. But the brand’s latest trick might be its riskiest: it’s attempting to sneak a drone past the FCC.

In December 2025, the US government banned future foreign drones from reaching our shores, and HoverAir’s floating Aqua drone was one of the first casualties. But this time, brand owner Zero Zero Robotics might have found a loophole: its latest $750 Versa drone isn’t a drone, it claims, but rather a DJI Pocket-style mini steadycam with optional snap-on propellers that let it fly.

Empty offices. Bald-faced lies. No real consequences. Our investigation suggests it’s cheap and easy to dodge the US drone ban — but at least the FCC is revoking Odyssey Robot’s authorizations after we made the agency aware. The FCC’s order doesn’t begin to explore whether Odyssey might be DJI in disguise.

No other gadget knows as much about your home as a robot vacuum. They map your space, learn your routines, and many now carry a camera and microphone into every room in your house. As AI gives them a better understanding of what they see, the privacy and security risks these autonomous robots pose are only growing.

That’s why greater scrutiny makes sense, but the ban on foreign-made mobile robots issued by the Federal Communications Commission earlier this week — implemented to address “unacceptable risks to the national security of the United States or the safety and security of United States persons” — doesn’t actually judge products by how secure they are, just where they’re built. It effectively means no new robot vacuums from major companies can enter the US market unless they are built here. And spoiler alert: none are.

When the Trump administration announced yesterday that it was banning “advanced robotic devices” from entering the United States, the headlines were all about humanoids. But spying doesn’t require legs — and neither does the FCC’s robot ban.

The robot ban will sweep up robot vacuum cleaners too, FCC media relations director Katie Gorscak confirms to The Verge.

If so, that would be new — the FCC proposed a $25,000 fine against Xtra for failing to respond to its letters, and has since revealed plans to ban Xtra from importing and selling more of its existing cameras in the US. Xtra might be canceling its new DJI Osmo Pocket 4 Pro variant, too.

After selling a barely disguised version of the hit DJI Osmo Pocket 3 in the United States last year, Xtra Technology seemed ready to sneak in its version of the new dual-lens Osmo Pocket 4 Pro, too. But today, Xtra has abruptly removed the new Xtra Muse 2 Pro from its website landing page, is pausing preorders indefinitely, and says it’ll refund all the $20 deposits that it’s already collected.

Just days after showing off the camera at a Los Angeles launch event, the company is no longer necessarily committing to launch at all: “XTRA has not made a final announcement regarding future product availability. We have not announced a new product launch timeline at this time. Further information will be shared separately once the relevant plans are confirmed,” it writes.

In Pasadena, California, there’s a cute red brick courtyard where one storefront isn’t like the rest. The glass doors open onto a sparse industrial hallway, which leads to a sunlit foyer with a large spiral staircase. Go up, and you’ll see a typical coworking space: open tables, healthy snacks, a collection of meeting rooms with glass walls. Follow one hallway most of the way back, and you’ll find Office 18.

There are no signs of life in this tiny private office that’s supposed to be the headquarters of Odyssey Robot, an upstart US drone company. We saw three small desks and chairs, but no power strips, no monitors, no keyboards or chargers or decorations. Two of the desks were covered with sealed cardboard packages.

Last October, we told you how the FCC had given itself the power to retroactively ban gadgets that have already received its approval to be imported and sold in the United States. Now, the FCC’s getting ready to wield that power for the first time, by cracking down on the “DJI front companies” suspected of sneaking the Chinese company’s tech past its foreign drone ban.

Two Fridays ago, the FCC had already proposed a $25,000 fine on eight of those “front companies,” including the ones behind the Skyrover drones and Xtra cameras. But now, the FCC is proposing a lot more than a slap on the wrist — it’s planning to ban those same companies from continuing to import, distribute, market, and sell their existing drones and cameras.

Last year, we told you about Xtra, the company that lets DJI sneak its popular cameras into the US, and Skyrover, a brand seemingly selling DJI drones in disguise. They’re just two of the many firms DJI is suspected of starting to skirt the United States’ foreign drone ban.

But it appears the FCC is finally doing something about “DJI front companies,” as watcher Konrad Iturbe dubbed them last year. Today, the FCC is fining eight of them $25,000 each — and giving them until Monday, July 20th, just 10 calendar days, to answer the FCC’s questions before the agency takes further action.

The FCC is giving Amazon conditional approval to import future routers into the US, following its decision to grant Netgear conditional approval earlier this month.

”We remain committed to delivering innovative, reliable products our customers can depend on,” Eero says.

It’s been 24 hours. I tried them again five hours ago too. Note that we did not ask if Netgear “bribed the government” to escape the router ban as readers keep suggesting, or anything like that. We simply asked if Netgear followed the FCC’s own rules by submitting a detailed plan for US manufacturing.

The United States’ foreign router ban didn’t make a whole lot of sense, and today may not change that. The FCC has just granted Netgear a conditional approval to import its future consumer routers, cable modems, and cable gateways into the US through October 1st, 2027 — even though the company builds those devices in Asia and has not announced any plan to bring manufacturing to the United States.

Neither the FCC’s announcement nor Netgear’s announcement explain why Netgear was granted the temporary exemption. The FCC only states that the Pentagon has now made “a specific determination” that “such devices do not pose risks to U.S. national security.”

#191. Pocket-sized point-and-shoot cameras are having a moment — these are the best options

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NBC Selected independently determines what we cover and recommend. When you click on or buy through our links, we earn a commission. Learn more.

Pocketable point-and-shoot cameras are having a serious moment. New and old models are selling out, with most camera manufacturers struggling to keep up with demand. The Fujifilm X100VI, a very popular camera on social media, took me five months to receive after placing my pre-order.

As a tech reporter (and a photography enthusiast), I test cameras year-round — I’ve tried over a dozen cameras to get. Below are some of the best point-and-shoot cameras, plus expert advice about what makes these cameras worthwhile.

Want more from NBC Selected? Sign up for our newsletter, The Selection, and shop smarter.

My top picks come from camera brands like Sony, Leica and Ricoh. Some options on this list were sent to me by the brands for testing purposes for this story, others were not.

I list key specifications I think you should know below each recommendation. If you find any of them confusing, you can learn more about the basics of photography, including terms like aperture, shutter speed and ISO, on sites like Adobe, MasterClass, Skillshare, and Expert Photography.

This is one of the highest quality, most fully-featured compact cameras on the market. It is also one of the most coveted — I received mine five months after I placed my pre-order.

It has the largest and most detailed image sensor on our list (by far), and can capture incredibly sharp, detailed photos. This is made easy with the advanced auto-focus system — the camera uses AI-based subject detection to automatically focus on birds, animals, cars and even the individual eyes of a person’s face. The built-in image stabilization also means your shaky hands are less likely to cause blurry photos.

It has multiple dials and switches for precise control over your photography and videography. One of my favorite settings is the 20 Fujifilm film simulation modes, which are essentially “looks” with their own color, saturation, shadow detail and other settings to mimic the look of literal Fuji film.

The list of useful features goes on and on and on, but the camera does have a few limitations. The Fujifilm X100VI is the largest and heaviest camera on our list — I can fit it in large coat pockets, not a pants pocket. It also does not have a zoom lens. Both make it less flexible as a travel camera than our picks from Sony and Leica. You can learn more in my Fujifilm X100VI review.

If you are looking to spend under $200, but want something with more control than an instant camera, consider this budget Kodak camera. Former NBC Selected associate reporter Bianca Alvarez took the Pixpro FZ55 abroad — it was so small and lightweight that she brought it everywhere without a second thought. It has a smaller sensor that lacks detail compared to our other picks, but it is one of the most affordable point-and-shoot cameras on the market. It also makes for a good gift to anyone looking to try out photography. Just note that it won’t perform very well in low-light environments.

Anyone looking for a compact camera that takes high-quality photos and doesn’t skimp on features should consider the Sony RX100 VII.

NBC News associate art director Kara Birnbaum says this is the best point-and-shoot camera she has ever used. The images look crisp and clean, even in dark lighting, especially compared to similarly compact cameras from Panasonic and Kodak. Plus, the small size makes it easy for Birnbaum to bring it with her everywhere and snap photos when inspiration strikes — something that’s harder to do with her other cameras.

If this sounds like your dream camera but is out of your budget, consider the Sony ZV-1 as an alternative — it is more streamlined for taking videos, not photos, but has a similar design and controls.

Former NBC Selected commerce editor Jordan Bowman has taken some of his best photos with Ricoh GR cameras — he brings one almost everywhere. After all, the best camera is the one you have with you.

It takes sharper, more detailed photos than nearly all of our other recommendations (except the Fujifilm X100VI). Much of that image quality is because of this camera’s large sensor, which captures more sharp detail and a wider range of light and shadows than our picks from Sony, Leica, Kodak and Panasonic.

That image quality comes at a cost — the camera has a fixed lens that cannot zoom in or out. But the fixed lens has a wide field of view that’s great for street photography and snapshot style photos, says Bowman.

While it looks like a regular point-and-shoot camera, the Canon Powershot V1 is a video camera at heart, with features largely focused on easily capturing video content. It has easy to understand touch screen controls (along with dials and buttons), snappy autofocus and an included microphone cover that helps muffle wind noise.

While it’s centered on video, it’s a decent photo camera too, especially for the price. Its larger- than-average image sensor means more detailed shots than many competitors (including our Kodak and Panasonic picks). Plus, unlike many cameras on this list, it has been consistently in-stock online since its launch in April 2025.

Let me be clear: The Kodak Charmera does not take “good” photos. They are often blurry and not very detailed. But this camera is more about the fun in using it than the quality of the photos. It’s a conversation starter and a great party activity: not something to pour your photographic talent into.

It’s absolutely tiny (smaller than Apple AirPods): you can attach it to a key ring using the built-in loop on the side of the camera. It takes photos, videos, has a few filter effects and a tiny built-in flash. It comes in seven styles, but you can’t pick which one you’ll get: it’s a blind box where you don’t know what you’ll get until you open it.

If you want to photograph subjects at a distance — think wildlife or architectural details, but don’t want to lug around a big camera, this is the model to get. It has an incredible zoom range of 720 millimeters, over triple that of the Sony RX100 VII. Plus it has image stabilization built-in to help mitigate camera shake, especially when zoomed in on far-off subjects.

I’ve tested previous models in the Lumix ZS series, and found them to deliver a good mix of image quality, ease of use and portability. While image quality can’t compete with our picks from Sony and Leica, neither zoom nearly as far as the Lumix ZS99 does.

Editor’s note: I’m testing the Panasonic Lumix L10 . I may update this pick after I finish testing.

The Leica D-Lux 8 is probably my favorite camera on this list. I love the sleek design, precise manual controls and short but useful zoom range, but it’s the pictures I took that won me over. The larger than average image sensor delivers some remarkable shots that rival photos I’ve taken on much larger mirrorless cameras. It has a robust automatic mode, but I found myself tinkering with the many clicky dials to lock-in my exposure and create more thought-out images. The same cannot be said for most other point-and-shoot cameras I’ve tried.

It’s pricier and less pocketable than most of our other top picks. It also does not have a built-in flash, but it does come with a small flash unit you can slot into the top of the camera.

More than 10 NBC Selected staffers tested the original Camp Snap camera, and it instantly won our hearts because of how easy it is to use and how fun it is to shoot with. It’s modeled after an old-school disposable point-and-shoot film camera: There are no settings to change, no screen to look at, it’s just a viewfinder, flash and a shutter button. The little LCD screen on the back also tells you how many photos you’ve taken — that’s it. The colorful, textured material on the front of the camera is attractive but also helps with grip, and there is a small thumb imprint on the back of the camera which helps you hold it as the camera is very rectangular.

The Camp Snap 2 improves upon the original in every way. You can now change between six built-in color filters with the button on the back, and the on-off switch and shutter button are now separate, which makes turning it on and off and actually taking photos much more responsive than on the original.

Unfortunately, the photos are still largely hit or miss. Our favorites were either taken outdoors with an abundance of light or inside with the flash on. You can learn more in my Camp Snap camera review.

If you need a durable camera that you can use in basically any environment, Mark Steinberg, the senior technologist at B&H Photo & Video, recommends the Olympus TG series. This TG-7 is the latest model and its designed for extreme conditions — you can use it underwater up to about 50 feet, according to the brand. Plus it’s shockproof up to seven feet and crushproof, able to resist up to 220 pounds of force. Upgrades from the previous TG-6 include a more color-accurate screen, a ribbed plastic texture around the grip, USB-C charging and more.

There is a tradeoff to its small size and durable build — it has a smaller, less detailed sensor compared to many of our top picks.

A point-and-shoot digital camera is typically small and compact, with a built-in lens and features like automatic photo and video modes, according to our experts. This is different from DSLR and mirrorless cameras, which can range in size and weight and have larger, removable lenses.

I focus on small and compact point-and-shoot cameras in this guide, but there are other kinds on the market too, including “super zoom” models with longer, bulkier lenses.

To pick the best point-and-shoot cameras on the market, I tried models from brands like Canon, Sony, Fujifilm, Kodak and Panasonic throughout the year. Then, to learn more about cameras and current trends, I spoke with Steinberg (note: B&H Photo & Video is one of the biggest camera stores in the U.S) and chose cameras based on the following criteria:

Cameras, even within a sub-category like point-and-shoot, can vary in shape, size and function. The Om System Tough TG-7 and Panasonic Lumix ZS99 may have a similar price tag, but they look, feel and function very differently. Below are a few things our experts recommend keeping in mind as you shop for your next camera.

A camera that’s great for taking family portraits may not also be great at photographing far-off wildlife. Before you purchase any camera, it’s important to understand what you are looking to photograph or film.

If you want to shoot photos of wildlife, look for a camera with a long zoom range — professional wildlife lenses typically have a minimum zoom length of at least 200mm, according to B&H Photo & Video. If you plan on mostly shooting video, consider choosing a camera with a flip-up screen that makes it easier to film yourself, and one that can shoot video in a 4K resolution. If you love the detail of a sunset or texture of flower petals, look for a camera with a larger image sensor, it’s going to capture those details with more clarity, in my experience.

I’ve tested enough cameras to know that sometimes it’s all about feel. Some cameras feel great in my hands, some don’t. Before purchasing your next camera, consider shopping in-person so you can physically hold and test your options, says Steinberg.

All digital cameras have an image sensor inside. When you take a photo, light goes through the lens and hits the digital image sensor inside, which creates the digital image. A bigger image sensor typically means a more detailed photo (especially in a darker environment) and a wider range of highlights and shadows (dynamic range).

But a larger image sensor usually means a bigger and more expensive camera. See the most common image sensor types below, listed from largest to smallest:

Full-frame (36x22mm). Includes most mirrorless cameras from Canon, Sony and Nikon, especially professional models.

APS-C (24x16mm). Includes the Fujifilm X100 series, Ricoh GR series and many mirrorless cameras from Fujifilm, Sony, Nikon and Canon.

Micro four thirds (17x13mm). Includes the Leica D-Lux 8 and many mirrorless cameras from OM System and Panasonic.

1-inch sensor (12.8x9.6mm). Includes the Sony RX series and Canon PowerShot series.

Less than 1- inch sensor. Includes smartphones, Kodak Pixpro series, Panasonic ZS series, OM System Tough series and more.

This is different from resolution or megapixels — cameras can have the same sized image sensor but a different amount of megapixel.

There are pros and cons to point-and-shoot cameras and phone cameras.

New, high-end phones may take “better” photos and videos than some point-and-shoot cameras, according to Steinberg.

However, if you are looking to use your photos outside of social media, look for a camera that can shoot raw image files, says photo editor Birnbaum. Raw files are the purest form of a digital image, and are much more detailed and well-suited to photo editing and printing. Most phone cameras cannot shoot raw image files, while most dedicated cameras can.

Point-and-shoot cameras are typically more ergonomic than phone cameras — they are easier to hold and easier to control, says Steinberg. That’s likely because phones were never designed with photography as their sole purpose.

Outside of models like the Om System Tough TG-7, most cameras aren’t totally weatherproof. By contrast, nearly all smartphones are, with high IP ratings to match.

Ultimately, our experts agree that the best camera is the one you have with you. “If you don’t have your camera ready, you miss your shot,” says Steinberg.

He tests new point-and-shoot cameras year-round, and spoke with other NBC Selected team members about their experiences with cameras they use frequently. He also spoke with photography and retail experts to better understand point-and-shoot cameras and their place in the market.

#250. Anthropic just launched tools to show companies what their AI spending is buying

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW.

The financials Anthropic shared with investors this week suggest that its AI coding tool, Claude Code, is every bit the business success the frontier lab wanted. The company recorded an operating profit in the second quarter, and 900% net revenue retention — that is, spending from the same customers in Q3 2025 grew nine times in Q3 2026.

Anthropic has focused on getting enterprise users to stick around and Wednesday announced a new suite of reporting tools designed to give those customers a more granular understanding of the value they get from their token spend.

Using internal analytics and employee surveys, managers can view a team’s work over a given date range, what the team produced, how much it cost, and where its workflows broke down. Anthropic says it won’t use this data for model training by default. The reports will also offer recommendations on how to improve the results.

This is a clear attempt to demonstrate the return on investment of Anthropic’s tools and to show customers how to improve it — a major concern among large customers. Anthropic is also creating pooled group budgets to allow teams to better manage token spending.

Businesses have been hunting for that kind of information as they figure out how best to deploy AI tools. While usage among venture-backed firms is driving token spend, most of the Fortune 500 are not seeing major gains from deploying AI.

Jellyfish, a company that makes tools for software developers, released a new report yesterday based on the use of agentic coding tools by 276,000 of its customers. Claude Code and other tools built on Anthropic models remain the most widely used in the survey, responsible for nearly a third of all proposed code changes submitted for review. More importantly, spending on those tools has grown 10x since the beginning of the year, with their cost now equal to about 2% of total engineering cost.

“Even if you’re saying 2% is still small, that 10x growth is growth that most CFOs didn’t plan for,” Nicholas Arcolano, the chief scientist at Jellyfish, told TechCrunch. We’re certainly getting to the point where people are realizing, okay, it’s not enough to just spend a bunch. You now have to optimize it, make it work.”

Anthropic’s new reports speak to that challenge. Anthropic leads among enterprise customers, but what makes an agentic coding tool truly sticky is difficult to identify. The release of OpenAI’s Astra model this summer seemed like a potential threat to Anthropic, but so far it hasn’t slowed Anthropic’s adoption numbers.

“It took a long time for us to see Codex really pop in our data at all, and it has come up with Astra [OpenAI’s latest model],” Nikolas Albarran, the AI product lead at Jellyfish, told TechCrunch. Looking at X, he continued, you’d get the distinct impression that the competition is more heated. “Everyone in the Valley is like, ‘Oh, Codex is better.’” In reality, Albarran continued, “Anthropic was just there with great enterprise motions, and they had an incumbent weight of their own — this stuff just worked and worked well, and a lot of people just put a lot of effort into standardizing workflows around it. And there’s not a big enough reason to switch.”

It’s not just a question of models and workflows, either. The realities of business mean that once a frontier lab has gotten its hooks into a customer, switching is harder than it is compared to an individual programmer or small organization. Albarran points to the investment all the major AI labs are making in forward-deployed engineering (FDE) organizations, teams that embed AI consultants inside customer organizations to help them get off the ground. These new reporting tools can be seen as a kind of FDE-lite, a lighter version of that hands-on help, for Anthropic customers.

“We’re at a point now where it’s mature enough, where once you’re in, it’s going to be harder and harder to switch because you’ve spent the effort,” he said. “Even if they’re all mostly the same, they’re all going to be slightly different, have their own slight quirks of how they handle agent.md files, etc., and so that cost to switch is not zero.”

We're looking for freelance journalists who know their way around a tech blog.

Engadget has been covering the tech world for over 20 years, but there are only so many stories we can write in a given week. As such, we're hoping to add some contributing writers to our team.

We're searching for writers who know their way around the tech space and can turn around fast, clean copy. You don't need to know every facet of every topic we cover, but if you don't have a broad familiarity with the consumer tech space, this probably isn't the role for you. More than anything, we're looking for writers with the ability to distill complex subjects into stories that anyone can understand. While this shouldn't be your first writing gig, these are entry-level roles, and we value deep knowledge and enthusiasm for tech higher than a professional degree or decades of experience.

Engadget has a strict no-AI policy. Writers need to have a mastery of the English language, which includes the ability to write accurate, engaging and informative stories without the assistance of AI.

Provided you're not a robot, you'll be working with some of the most knowledgeable and experienced folks in tech journalism. Our news and features desks provide a steady stream of daily assignments, so there will always be fresh articles ready for you to tackle each day. Once you're up and running with a few articles under your belt, you'll be free to pitch and develop your own ideas, too.

Engadget is an entirely remote workplace. Our team is spread across four continents and can accommodate writers in any timezone. We offer either flat hourly or per-word rates, with the exact figures varying on experience. Any time you spend with Engadget, including training or trials, will be paid.

We pride ourselves on providing a friendly, collaborative and respectful working environment, with regular hours, regular assignments and regular pay. Many of our writers have been with us for years-– several of them over a decade — so we must be doing something right.

If the chance to have your work seen by the millions of readers who come to Engadget every month sounds like a dream, you can apply for a role here.

#255. Reed Hastings says Big Tech should get used to being hated — like Big Pharma

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Reed Hastings thinks the tech industry has some Big Pharma energy.

During an interview with "On with Kara Swisher," released on Monday, the Netflix founder and former co-CEO responded to questions about the unpopularity of Silicon Valley executives.

"Well, think of pharma," he said. "Pharma is hated by the general public, and to my eyes, is continuing to do amazing and positive work. But people hate the high drug prices, and so pharma is deeply hated."

Hastings also said that drug companies have developed "remarkable" products — like the COVID vaccines — but still draw public ire.

"Did people fall in love with those companies? They did not."

He also compared tech execs to banking and airline bosses: powerful, consequential, and largely disliked.

Swisher raised the question as Hollywood is highlighting some of tech's ugliest chapters in movie theaters across America. A24's "You Can See Everything" has blown up the box office — it's a documentary following former Theranos CEO Elizabeth Holmes in the weeks before she reports to prison. Aaron Sorkin's "The Social Reckoning" dramatizes whistleblowing at Meta. Alex Gibney's "Musk" tracks the rise of Elon Musk's political influence. And Luca Guadagnino's "Artificial," a biopic about a biopic that focuses on OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, is out on Christmas.

Hastings said that tech's yearslong tumble out of public favor is not particularly unusual. Large industries always invite skepticism.

"That's the natural state of how much of the public thinks about business," he said.

Still, Hastings, who sits on Anthropic's board, said that Silicon Valley companies still have one potential PR golden goose.

"We've got to actually cure some diseases," he said. "That'll help."

#292. Bond investor Jeff Gundlach on Fed's rate pause: 'What are you waiting for?'

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

DoubleLine founder Jeff Gundlach told CNBC's "Closing Bell" on Wednesday that it's unclear why the Federal Reserve is waiting to hike rates further.

"What are you waiting for? You just upgraded the economy right here," he said, referring to Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh's comments about a strong macroeconomic environment during the central bank's post-decision news conference. "We're headed to 4% handle headline CPI. The commodity index is rising in a bull market, and I don't understand why the Fed is so reluctant to move."

His comments come after the central bank decided to leave interest rates unchanged at its October meeting.

Following the Fed's decision to hike rates by 25 basis points at its September meeting, Gundlach told CNBC that the central bank should have raised interest rates by even more. He argued back then that the bank needed to raise rates by 50 basis points, rather than hike them gradually over various meetings.

"I just think that the Fed is a little bit behind the curve right now," he said Wednesday. Gundlach noted that the Fed's 3.75%-4% overnight rate is significantly below that of the 2-year Treasury note, which trades around 4.7%. That is a sign the Fed needs to hike more than it already has, he said.

Despite holding rates steady, there is still an 85% chance the Fed raises interest rates by 25 basis points at its December meeting, according to CME's FedWatch tool. The Fed said in a statement it will use further information to determine the correct policy to ensure a return to the Fed's inflation goal of 2%, though Warsh at a press conference reiterated future policy decisions can't be determined at this time.

"Absent a crack in the oil price, I think they have to hike in December," Gundlach said. U.S. oil prices continued to trade above $100 per barrel on Wednesday.

He also said his investment strategy remains unchanged after the decision, with 30% of his portfolio allocated to equities — where he particularly favors the equal-weight S&P 500 to avoid too much exposure to what he calls potential artificial intelligence "problems" — 30% to bonds, 20% toward commodities and 20% in "dry powder."

Warsh also announced that one meeting in 2027 and two in 2028 of the scheduled eight Fed gatherings will focus on policy strategy, and no vote on the fed funds rate will take place. Gundlach said the move wasn't surprising, adding he was in favor of having only six rate-setting meetings starting immediately.

"I don't think eight meetings is really necessary," he said. "It seems like they come up really quickly ... at least you have a couple months worth of data to filter in."

#406. The New M6 MacBook Pro Has a Serious Price Problem

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Just yesterday, Apple refreshed its 14-inch MacBook Pro with the new M6 chip inside, and it’s currently available for preorder. This is the first MacBook to get the chip (but certainly not the last), and I look forward to testing this thing out myself. But I do already have a good sense of how well it’ll perform, as I already tested this same chip in the latest Mac Mini. And it points to an issue with the current state of the Mac lineup.

The 14-inch M6 MacBook Pro currently costs $1,999. That gets you 16 GB of RAM and 512 GB of storage. Even for as good as these laptops are, that feels like too much money for those specs. Apple raised prices across all Macs earlier this year, but the base 14-inch MacBook Pro got hit the hardest, with a $300 bump. It was already an awkward configuration, as it used the same M5 chip as the much cheaper MacBook Air models. It was such bad pricing that it ended up on my “do not buy” list. The addition of the M6 seems like it’ll add some value, especially with the improvements to GPU performance I saw in the M6 Mac Mini. It also has the dual Neural Engine, which can speed up certain AI tasks in creative applications, especially for content creators. I won’t pretend to make a final evaluation until I’ve thoroughly tested the machine myself.

But consider this. The M6 Mac Mini is on sale for $779 right now, $120 off its retail price. The 13-inch M5 MacBook Air is similarly $150 off its retail price right now. You could buy both for the same price as one M6 MacBook Pro. That’s kind of insane when you think about it. You miss out on the MacBook Pro’s excellent Mini-LED display and powerful speakers, but to me, it’s a tempting alternative for a $2,000 budget.

It’s even more compelling when you consider how many people are using Mac Minis these days—more as always-on AI agent boxes that you can control remotely with any old MacBook. Pairing the M6 Mac Mini and M5 MacBook air together is a recipe for AI goodness (especially if you configure the Mac Mini up to 32 GB or 64 GB of memory).

Another alternative is the M5 MacBook Pro with an M5 Pro chip inside for only $200 more. It looks identical on the outside, and has better performance on the inside.

I’m still interested to see how the new M6 MacBook Pro performs, but the price alone makes me wonder if there aren’t better ways of spending your money—even while staying within the Mac lineup.
